    Design kloudi's agent loop:                                                 
                                                                                
    1. AI-NATIVE: LLM is control flow. SOP graph is directed path. Min          
  branches.                                                                     
    2. TWO LOOPS: Outer (conversational) + inner (SOP execution). Inner =       
  current execution-engine but                                                  
    AI-native.                                                                  
                                                                                
    3. THREE MODES as configurations: Operating (interactive+background),       
  Onboarding (research+evaluation),                                             
    Learning (scoring+threshold).                                               
                                                                                
    4. CONTEXT ASSEMBLY: Graph tells LLM what context to read and when          
  (write-erd example).                                                          
    5. BACKLINKS: SOPs reference each other. Loop uses backlinks for traversal. 
                                                                                
    6. PATTERNS: Pi (EventStream, steering), OpenCode (pubsub), Goose (MCP      
  types), Hermes (failover, iteration                                           
    budget), Open Agents (durable workflows).                                   
                                                                                
    7. Vercel AI SDK ToolLoopAgent for LLM call layer.

⏺ docs/agent-loop-design.md is written — 16 sections, 8 ASCII diagrams.

  Here's what the design resolves:

  The core shift: The current ExecutionEngine dispatches on node.type
  (executors.get(node.type)). The AI-native version eliminates that entirely —
  the LLM is the executor at every step, graph nodes become context assembly
  declarations, and the LLM reasons freely about how to complete each step.

  Two loops, hard boundary:
  - Outer: conversational, mode-aware, handles steering messages (Pi pattern),
  session lifecycle (OpenCode pattern)
  - Inner: SOP execution — context assembly → LLM turn → tool loop → node
  advance

  Key borrowed patterns and why:
  - Pi: EventStream, steering messages for mid-flight user input,
  sequential/parallel tool execution
  - OpenCode: session-busy detection (activeRequests map), pubsub broker for
  fan-out to terminal/feed/Postgres
  - Goose: inspector pipeline (Security → Egress → Trust → Repetition), MCP
  extension types (builtin/stdio/HTTP)
  - Hermes: independent iteration budgets per sub-SOP (shared budgets cause
  surprise exhaustion), error-classified provider failover, refund for read-only
   turns

  Six open questions flagged at the end — the biggest unsettled one is the
  background job mechanism (pg-boss rejected, Temporal is overkill, Render cron
  is the current lean recommendation).

⏺ Good picture of what exists. Let me explain this clearly.

  ---
  What exists today (current → database)

  The flow for the core action — running a SOP — is:

  User (web/CLI)
    │
    │  POST /api/sops/:id/run
    ▼
  Express route (executions.routes.ts)
    │  looks up SOP in Postgres
    │  calls engine.execute()
    │  returns 202 { executionId }  ← user gets this immediately
    ▼
  ExecutionEngine.runGraph()  [runs async via setImmediate]
    │
    │  LOOP: for each graph node
    │    ├── writes ExecutionNode { status: 'running' } → Postgres
    │    ├── dispatches to typed executor (LLMExecutor | ToolCallExecutor |
  etc.)
    │    ├── if trust gate → pauses loop, fires WebSocket to browser, waits
    │    ├── writes ExecutionNode { status: 'completed', output } → Postgres
    │    └── continues to next node
    │
    └── writes Execution { status: 'completed' } → Postgres

  WebSocket (parallel)
    │  streams progress events to browser as nodes complete
    │  trust gate: browser sends approve/reject → loop resumes

  Database touched: sops (read), executions (create + updates), execution_nodes
  (create per node + update)

  ---
  The two use cases that show the full breadth of changes

  Use case 1 — User runs write-erd interactively

  Today:
  POST /api/sops/write-erd/run
  → engine looks at graph.nodes
  → node[0].type === 'llm_generate' → LLMExecutor runs it
  → node[1].type === 'tool_call' → ToolCallExecutor runs it
  → node[2].type === 'llm_generate' → LLMExecutor again
  → results written to execution_nodes
  → done

  The graph is a program. The engine executes it deterministically. The LLM is
  called inside the executor, not as the executor.

  After the agent loop design:
  CLI: "run write-erd for beacon feature X"
  → outer loop LLM call: identifies the SOP, decides to call run_sop tool
  → inner loop starts: loads graph from sops table
  → node[0]: context_sources = [product/draft-prd]
      engine fetches latest ExecutionNode output for draft-prd from Postgres
      adds to context window
  → LLM turn: "Here's the PRD. Here's your task. You have write_file,
  read_file."
  → LLM calls read_file(schema/) → result → LLM calls write_file(erd/schema.md)
      trust inspector: write_file is 'prompt' level → pause
      stream trust gate to terminal → user types y
      write_file executes
  → LLM says end_turn → node complete → node[1] starts
  → execution_nodes written throughout

  The graph is context assembly instructions. The LLM is the executor.

  Database changes needed for this:
  - sops.graph nodes change shape: from { type: 'llm_generate', config: { 
  prompt_template } } to { description: '...', context_sources: [...], 
  available_tools: [...], trust_required: 'prompt' }
  - executions needs: sessionId, mode, iterationBudget, iterationsUsed (new
  columns)
  - execution_nodes needs: contextTokens, contextSources (what was assembled),
  trustDecision
  - New table needed: sessions (agent sessions, separate from auth sessions) —
  tracks outer loop state, activeRequests, steering messages queue
  - New table needed: trust_scores (userId, actionType, orgId →
  auto/prompt/block) — replaces hardcoded requiresApproval on nodes

  ---
  Use case 2 — Background mode, webhook arrives

  Today: Nothing. Background mode doesn't exist. The engine only runs when POST 
  /api/sops/:id/run is called by a user.

  After:
  GitHub webhook: PR opened
  → Observer receives event (new)
  → Outer loop triggered: no terminal, mode = background
  → LLM: "PR #42 opened by Alice. Relevant SOP: engineering/review-pr."
  → inner loop runs review-pr graph
  → trust gate hit (post comment on GitHub): trust_required = 'prompt'
      no terminal → can't ask user inline
      → writes FeedEvent to Postgres { type: 'approval_needed', executionId,
  nodeId, preview }
      → Execution.status = 'waiting_input'
      → loop pauses, survives server restart
  → User sees card in web Feed: "I reviewed PR #42. Post this comment?"
  [Approve] [Reject]
  → User clicks Approve → POST /api/executions/:id/resume
  → outer loop resumes from Postgres state, continues where it paused

  Database changes needed for this:
  - New table: feed_events (executionId, orgId, type, content, actionButtons,
  status: pending/actioned)
  - executions.status = 'waiting_input' already exists — but currentNodeId must
  survive restart (it does already)
  - New route: POST /api/executions/:id/resume with { decision: 'approve' | 
  'reject' } — doesn't exist today
  - executions needs: triggeredBy field — 'user' | 'webhook' | 'scheduler' (so
  feed can show the right context)

  ---
  Summary of what's changing

  ┌───────────────┬───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Layer     │         Today         │              After              │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ POST                  │ Same route + webhook receiver + │
  │ Entry point   │ /api/sops/:id/run     │  scheduler tick                 │
  │               │ (user only)           │                                 │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ ExecutionEngine       │ Outer loop (conversational) +   │
  │ Orchestration │ (deterministic node   │ inner loop (LLM-as-executor)    │
  │               │ dispatch)             │                                 │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ Called inside         │                                 │
  │ LLM role      │ executor per node     │ IS the executor at every step   │
  │               │ type                  │                                 │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ requiresApproval flag │ Inspector pipeline → terminal   │
  │ Trust gates   │  on tool_call nodes → │ (interactive) or feed card      │
  │               │  WebSocket            │ (background)                    │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ Not implemented (loop │ POST /api/executions/:id/resume │
  │ Resume        │  dies if trust gate   │  + Postgres state survives      │
  │               │ has no callback)      │ restart                         │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Session       │ No session concept    │ sessions table — tracks         │
  │               │ for agent             │ activeRequests, mode, budget    │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Background    │ Doesn't exist         │ Observer + background           │
  │               │                       │ MachineConfig + feed            │
  └───────────────┴───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  Two new tables minimum: feed_events, agent_sessions
  Two columns on executions: triggeredBy, iterationsUsed
  One new route: POST /api/executions/:id/resume
  Graph node schema: changes, but stored in same sops.graph jsonb column

  The existing Execution + ExecutionNode tables are fine as-is — they're the
  trace store and don't need structural changes, just a few added columns. The
  biggest new thing is feed_events (the background mode output channel) and
  agent_sessions (the outer loop state store).

  Would you like me to go deeper on any of these two use cases, or sketch the
  exact schema for the two new tables?

✻ Churned for 1m 16s

❯ sops are equivalent to processes in org, how do we plan to use canvas like    
  n8n and an engine like temporal. Am I thinking that correctyly. Is there any  
  existing sop tool that exist in the market that we can use?                   

⏺ Good questions. Let me answer all three directly.

  ---
  SOPs = org processes. Yes, exactly.

  A SOP in kloudi is the same thing as a business process — "how we write an
  ERD," "how we review a PR," "how we run a postmortem." The word SOP is just
  more precise than "process" because it implies a standard that can be followed
   consistently, not just a rough description. So your mental model is right.

  ---
  n8n canvas + Temporal engine — partially right, but with an important 
  difference

  The canvas (n8n): Yes. The Graph Editor in the OS is conceptually n8n's canvas
   — visual node/edge authoring, the output is a JSON graph. The .graph.json
  file is structurally the same thing as an n8n workflow JSON. React Flow
  (already in the candidate tech list) is exactly what you'd build that with.
  n8n itself is a good design reference for UX, not something to adopt — it's
  built for deterministic integrations (HTTP node, database node), not for
  context assembly instructions that an LLM reasons about.

  The engine (Temporal): This is the honest nuance. Temporal would solve real
  problems we currently have:

  ┌───────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │      Problem      │        Current hack        │   Temporal gives you   │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Durable execution │ Postgres status =          │ First-class durable    │
  │  across restarts  │ waiting_input + manual     │ workflows              │
  │                   │ state                      │                        │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Trust gate        │                            │ Signals (external      │
  │ pausing           │ Polling + WebSocket        │ input to running       │
  │                   │                            │ workflow)              │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Sub-SOP calls     │ executeAndWait() nested    │ Child workflows        │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Background        │ TBD (pg-boss was rejected) │ Built-in scheduler +   │
  │ scheduling        │                            │ cron                   │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Retry with        │ Manual try/catch           │ Activity retry         │
  │ backoff           │                            │ policies               │
  └───────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  The execution engine we've hand-rolled in Postgres is a simplified, fragile
  version of what Temporal does natively. The "background job mechanism" open
  question from the design doc is essentially "do we build toward Temporal or
  not."

  The reason to be cautious: Temporal Cloud is expensive. Self-hosted Temporal
  adds ops burden on a free tier Render setup. For Phase 1 it's overkill. But
  architecturally, the inner loop's runGraph() is a Temporal workflow in
  everything but name — it has activities (nodes), signals (trust gates), child
  workflows (sub-SOPs), and durable state. Worth keeping that shape in mind even
   if we don't adopt Temporal until Phase 3.

  ---
  Existing SOP tools in the market

  These are the relevant players, grouped by what they actually do:

  Documentation-only (no execution):
  - Process Street — closest to kloudi's SOP concept. Checklists, conditional
  branching, form fields. People follow the steps manually. No automation, no
  AI. 100k+ companies use this.
  - Trainual — employee training + SOPs. Content-first. No execution.
  - SweetProcess — same category as Process Street.
  - Confluence — where most orgs document SOPs today. Just markdown.

  Visual automation (execution, but not org-knowledge-aware):
  - n8n — open source, visual workflow builder. The canvas UX reference. Nodes
  are deterministic integrations. No concept of organizational knowledge or AI
  reasoning.
  - Zapier / Make — same category, hosted.
  - Pipefy — process management with visual forms. More human-in-the-loop than
  automated.

  Enterprise process engines (heavyweight):
  - Camunda — BPMN canvas + execution engine. This is actually the closest
  technical analogue to what kloudi is building on the engine side. Open source
  core. But it's built for deterministic code paths, not LLM reasoning.
  Enterprise Java ecosystem.
  - Nintex / Appian / ServiceNow — large enterprises, no AI, expensive, heavy.

  The gap none of them fill:

  Every existing tool is either documentation (humans follow steps) or
  deterministic automation (code follows steps). None of them:
  - Assemble organizational context before each step so the LLM has what it
  needs
  - Use the LLM as the executor rather than as a node type
  - Learn from execution traces to improve the SOPs themselves
  - Generate SOPs by observing how the org already works (onboarding mode)

  Process Street is the best existing product for SOP management and worth
  studying for UX. Camunda is the best existing product for the execution engine
   and worth studying for the workflow model. Neither is worth adopting — the
  AI-native layer is exactly what makes kloudi different from both.

  ---
  Bottom line: Your instinct about n8n + Temporal is structurally right. The
  canvas is React Flow shaped like n8n. The engine is Temporal-shaped but
  hand-rolled in Postgres for now, with a migration path to actual Temporal if
  durability becomes a real problem post-Phase 1. No existing SOP tool is worth
  adopting — the combination of organizational knowledge + AI-native execution
  is the product.

✻ Brewed for 1m 6s

❯ "how we write an ERD," "how we review a PR," "how                             
    we run a postmortem."                                                       
                                                                                
  not only this but brds, prds, erds and tech docs, test cases are to be        
  treated as sops for writing software or a feature don'y you think?            

⏺ Yes. And this is actually a more precise way to think about it than what the
  design doc currently captures.

  There are two kinds of SOPs:

  Process SOPs — "how we do things"
  - engineering/write-erd — how we create an ERD
  - engineering/review-pr — how we review a PR
  - product/draft-prd — how we write a PRD

  Artifact SOPs — "what we produce"
  - The PRD itself — structured document with defined sections (problem, user
  stories, success metrics, acceptance criteria)
  - The ERD itself — schema + migration plan + constraints
  - The BRD, tech spec, test plan, ADR

  The key insight is they're the same model. A PRD is not just a document that
  gets dumped in a folder. It's:

  1. The output of running product/draft-prd process SOP
  2. A structured artifact with its own required sections and completeness
  criteria
  3. The input context for engineering/write-erd via backlink
  4. Evaluatable — can be scored for quality before the next SOP proceeds

  So the flow is:

  product/draft-prd (process)
      → runs → produces →
          projects/beacon/prd/v2.md (artifact SOP)
              → backlinked into →
                  engineering/write-erd (process)
                      → runs → produces →
                          projects/beacon/erd/schema.md (artifact SOP)
                              → backlinked into →
                                  engineering/impl (process)
                                  engineering/qa (process)

  Every artifact in that chain is a first-class participant. This is exactly
  what "quality compounds through the chain" means concretely — a weak PRD
  produces a weak ERD produces a weak implementation, because each SOP assembles
   the previous artifact as context.

  The implication for the schema: Everything maps to the same Sop table. The
  level field (guide, project, task) already gestures at this but not precisely.
   The cleaner taxonomy is:

  ┌──────────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │  level   │          meaning          │             example              │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ process  │ how to do something       │ engineering/write-erd            │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ artifact │ a structured document     │ prd, erd, brd, test-plan,        │
  │          │ that gets produced        │ tech-spec, adr                   │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ template │ the blank form for an     │ templates/PRD-template.md        │
  │          │ artifact                  │                                  │
  └──────────┴───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Artifact SOPs have graphs too — not execution steps, but section graphs: the
  required parts of the document, their order, their validation rules, and what
  makes each section "complete enough" to proceed.

  This is also what makes onboarding mode powerful. When kloudi scans a new org,
   it doesn't just generate process SOPs — it generates artifact SOPs from the
  existing PRDs, ERDs, and tech specs it finds. It reverse-engineers both "how
  you do things" and "what your documents should look like." That's what makes
  the generated SOPs a lossless representation of how the org works.

  So yes — your instinct is right. Broaden the definition: a SOP is any unit of
  organizational knowledge that can be authored, executed, backlinked,
  evaluated, and improved. Processes and artifacts are both SOPs. The
  distinction is just in what the graph nodes mean — steps to follow vs sections
   to complete.

✻ Crunched for 48s

❯ The                                                                           
    distinction is just in what the graph nodes mean — steps to follow vs       
  sections to complete.                                                         
  explain / expand this a bit more                                              

⏺ The graph structure is the same. The node semantics are different.

  ---
  Process SOP — nodes are verbs. Things that happen.

  engineering/write-erd graph:

    [read_prd] ──→ [read_current_schema] ──→ [generate_erd] ──→ [write_file]
                                                  ↑
                                           trust_required: prompt

  Each node is an action. The LLM executes it, uses tools, moves on. The graph
  is temporal — things happen in order. A node is "done" when the action
  completes.

  ---
  Artifact SOP — nodes are nouns. Things that exist.

  PRD artifact graph:

    [problem_statement] ──→ [user_stories] ──→ [success_metrics] ──→
  [acceptance_criteria] ──→ [sign_off]

  Each node is a section of the document. The LLM fills it in. The graph is
  structural — it describes what the document must contain and in what order
  sections build on each other. A node is "done" when the section is complete
  enough.

  ---
  The key difference: what "done" means

  For a process node, done is binary. File written. API called. Review posted.
  It either happened or it didn't.

  For an artifact node, done is a quality threshold:

  node: user_stories
    validation:
      min_stories: 3
      format: each story has As a / I want / So that
      coverage: happy path + at least one edge case
      completeness_score: >= 0.80

    if score < 0.80:
      → LLM tries again with richer context
      → or flags the section as incomplete for human review

  This means artifact SOPs can loop within a section — keep trying until the
  section meets the bar, or surface it to the user with a specific gap
  identified ("the success metrics have no quantitative targets").

  ---
  But the mechanics are identical

  Both node types have the same fields:

  context_sources   — what to read before this step/section
  description       — natural language instruction for the LLM
  available_tools   — what the LLM can use
  trust_required    — who needs to approve this
  successors        — what comes next

  The same inner loop runs both. The LLM assembles context, reasons about the
  instruction, uses tools if needed, produces output. The only thing that
  differs is how the output is evaluated before advancing to the next node.

  ---
  The other thing artifact nodes give you: re-runs

  A process SOP runs once and produces a result. An artifact SOP produces a
  living document. When requirements change you don't start over — you re-run
  specific nodes:

  requirements changed for beacon feature X
    → re-run [success_metrics] node with new context
    → graph knows: [acceptance_criteria] depends on [success_metrics]
    → re-run [acceptance_criteria] automatically
    → downstream backlink: engineering/write-erd depends on this PRD
    → flag: "ERD may be stale — re-run recommended"

  This is the backlink system doing real work. The graph tells you the
  dependency order, so a change in one section propagates its staleness forward
  through the document and then outward to downstream process SOPs.

  ---
  Concrete comparison side by side

  ┌──────────────┬───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │              │        Process SOP        │        Artifact SOP         │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Example      │ engineering/write-erd     │ projects/beacon/prd/v2      │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Node =       │ a step to execute         │ a section to fill           │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Node done    │ action completes          │ quality threshold met       │
  │ when         │                           │                             │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Can loop?    │ only on error/retry       │ yes, until section passes   │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Output       │ a file, an API call, a    │ a section of the document   │
  │              │ result                    │                             │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Re-runnable? │ run again from scratch    │ re-run specific sections    │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM role     │ decides how to execute    │ decides what belongs in the │
  │              │ the step                  │  section                    │
  └──────────────┴───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  ---
  The same execution engine, same context assembly, same trust inspector
  pipeline runs both. You don't need two different systems. The only addition
  for artifact SOPs is a validation block on each node that defines what
  "complete enough" means for that section before the graph advances.

※ recap: Designing kloudi's AI-native agent loop where SOPs cover both processes and artifact documents like PRDs and ERDs. Next: decide whether to sketch the revised schema capturing both SOP types before moving to implementation. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ for an org to exists, people, process, tools and things people generate in    
  our case software this is the most important thing?                           

⏺ Yes. And it's worth being precise about why.

  The classic framework is People → Process → Tools. You're adding a fourth:
  Artifacts — what people actually produce. In a software org that's code, PRDs,
   ERDs, specs, ADRs, test plans, architecture docs.

  Here's why artifacts end up being the most important element for kloudi
  specifically:

  They're the only thing that persists.

  People leave. Processes drift from what's documented. Tools get replaced. But
  the artifacts accumulate. The code, the specs, the decisions — if captured —
  outlast everything else. A three-year-old ERD still tells you why the schema
  is shaped the way it is. A five-year-old ADR still tells you why you chose
  Postgres over MongoDB.

  They're the evidence of how the org actually works.

  This is the key insight for onboarding mode. Most orgs don't have good process
   documentation. But they have artifacts — Jira tickets, Confluence pages,
  GitHub PRs, Slack threads, existing code. The artifacts are the archaeological
   record of the actual processes, not the aspirational ones.

  So the flow runs in both directions:

  Forward (how work happens):
    Process SOP → runs → produces → Artifact

  Reverse (how Machine learns):
    Artifact → analyzed → reveals → Process SOP

  When kloudi scans a new org it doesn't ask "what are your processes?" It reads
   the artifacts and reverse-engineers the processes from the evidence. The PRDs
   reveal how product thinks. The ERDs reveal how engineering models data. The
  PR history reveals what the review culture actually is, not what the review
  checklist says it should be.

  For kloudi this means artifacts are the primary data, not the secondary 
  output.

  The four elements and what kloudi does with each:

  ┌───────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Element  │                 What kloudi models                  │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ People    │ User profiles, trust levels, department/tier        │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Process   │ Process SOPs (guides/)                              │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tools     │ Integrations (Jira, GitHub, Slack via MCP)          │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Artifacts │ Artifact SOPs (projects/) — PRDs, ERDs, specs, code │
  └───────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  But artifacts are the connective tissue between all the others. They're what
  processes produce, what tools store, and what people consume to do their next
  piece of work. Remove artifacts and the other three elements have no way to
  coordinate across time.

  The completeness test from the session summary makes more sense in this frame:

  ▎ "If you deleted everything else and used only the SOPs, the org's 
  ▎ operational configuration could be reconstructed."

  That only holds if SOPs include both process SOPs (how) and artifact SOPs
  (what). The process tells you how to write an ERD. The artifact is the ERD.
  Together they're complete. The process alone is instructions without evidence.
   The artifact alone is output without context.

  So yes — for a software org, the artifacts are the most important thing kloudi
   needs to model well. The processes are the verbs. The artifacts are the
  nouns. The org's knowledge lives in both, but the artifacts are what you can
  actually point to, evaluate, backlink, and compound quality through.

⏺ Let me read the design prototypes and key docs first.

⏺ Let me give you the mental model in layers, simple to complete.

  ---
  The one sentence version

  The Machine watches your org's tools, knows your org's processes, and reaches 
  out to the right person at the right moment — the same way Person of 
  Interest's Machine watches the city and sends numbers to its operatives.

  ---
  The Person of Interest mapping

  ┌────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          PoI: The Machine          │        kloudi: The Machine         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Watches cameras, phones, financial │ Watches GitHub, Jira, Slack, CI/CD │
  │  transactions                      │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Harold Finch — medium trust, gets  │ PM / manager — web UI,             │
  │ numbers via payphone               │ approves/reviews                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Root — high trust, earpiece, gets  │ Engineer — CLI terminal, executes  │
  │ full instructions                  │ directly                           │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Shaw, Reese — field operatives who │ The agent (CLI) running on your    │
  │  act                               │ machine                            │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sends a "number" when something    │ Posts to feed: "PR #42 opened, I   │
  │ needs attention                    │ have a plan"                       │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Finch builds and teaches The       │ You and your team author +         │
  │ Machine                            │ validate SOPs                      │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The Machine learns from every      │ Learning Mode improves SOPs from   │
  │ mission                            │ execution traces                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ One brain, multiple operatives     │ One server (brain), CLI per        │
  │                                    │ engineer (hands)                   │
  └────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  The crucial PoI insight that makes this click: The Machine doesn't wait to be 
  asked. It observes, it notices, it reaches out. You don't open kloudi and say
  "run write-erd." The Machine says "ACME-456 was assigned to you at 7am. I have
   a plan based on engineering/impl. Want to review it?"

  ---
  The org as a system The Machine watches

  An org is four things:

  PEOPLE          → who does what, what trust level, what department
  PROCESS         → how we work (guides/: write-erd, review-pr, run-postmortem)
  TOOLS           → GitHub, Jira, Slack, CI/CD, Confluence
  ARTIFACTS       → PRDs, ERDs, specs, code, test plans, ADRs

  The Machine's job is to hold all four together. Today, nobody does this. The
  PM writes the PRD in Confluence and puts a Jira link in it. The engineer
  writes the ERD in Notion and forgets to link the PRD. The code gets written
  without anyone reading the ERD properly. Quality doesn't compound — it leaks
  at every handoff.

  The Machine closes those leaks. It knows the PRD exists. It knows the ERD
  depends on the PRD. When you start write-erd, it assembles both before the LLM
   touches anything.

  ---
  The three surfaces = three trust levels

  CLI (terminal)       =  Root      Full execution. Local tools. Direct access.
                                    Engineer runs: kloudi → Machine drives work.

  Web UI               =  Finch     Review. Approve. See what happened.
                                    PM opens feed: Machine ran review-pr
  overnight,
                                    "approve this comment?" [Yes] [Edit] [No]

  Slack                =  Payphone  Notifications only. Simple approvals.
                                    "CI failed on develop. I found the cause.
                                     Want me to fix? [Yes] [Show me first]"

  The same Machine. Different trust, different interface. An engineer at the CLI
   gets the full picture and can run anything. A PM on web sees summaries and
  approves. Everyone on Slack gets a nudge when they're needed.

  ---
  The knowledge layer: SOPs are the org's DNA

  The Machine knows how your org works because SOPs encode it. Two kinds:

  PROCESS SOPs (guides/)          ARTIFACT SOPs (projects/)
  "how we do things"              "what we produce"

  guides/engineering/
    write-erd.md                  projects/beacon/
    review-pr.md                    prd/v2.md        ← output of draft-prd
    run-postmortem.md               erd/schema.md    ← output of write-erd
                                    test-plan.md     ← output of qa-sign-off
  guides/product/
    draft-prd.md

  The process SOPs are the verbs. The artifact SOPs are the nouns. Together
  they're complete. The Machine runs draft-prd (verb) → produces prd/v2.md
  (noun) → feeds it into write-erd (verb) → produces erd/schema.md (noun).
  Quality compounds through the chain because every step has full context from
  the previous one.

  ---
  What a day actually feels like

  8:00am — You open the terminal: kloudi

    Good morning, Nitish.

    Overnight:
    ✓ 3 PRs reviewed on fdx-service-pim (I left comments, waiting your approval)
    ✓ CI fixed itself on develop (flaky test, not your change)
    ⚠ ACME-456 assigned to you at 7:15am

    ACME-456: "Add SQL safety pre-check to migration flow"
    I have a plan based on engineering/impl + engineering/db-migrate.
    Want to review it? [yes/no]

    > yes

    ┌─ PLAN: ACME-456 ──────────────────────────────┐
    │  1. Read ACME-456 Jira ticket + linked ERD     │
    │  2. Read current migration code                │
    │  3. Write the safety check function            │
    │  ⚠ 4. Apply migration (trust gate)            │
    │  5. Write tests                                │
    │  6. Create PR                                  │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Steps 1-3, 5-6: auto (your trust level)
    Step 4: requires your approval

    > approve

    [1] Reading ACME-456... ✓
    [2] Reading migration code... ✓
    [3] Writing safety check... ✓
    ⚠ Trust gate: applying migration. Non-destructive. Approve? y
    [4] Migration applied ✓
    [5] Writing tests... ✓
    [6] PR #48 created ✓

  You didn't open Jira. You didn't search Confluence for the ERD. You didn't
  remember which migration file was relevant. The Machine knew all of it. You
  just approved and watched.

  ---
  What it's NOT

  - Not a chatbot. You don't ask it questions. It notices things and proposes.
  - Not a script runner. You don't click "run SOP." It identifies the right SOP
  for the situation.
  - Not Notion/Confluence. Those are passive — you go to them. The Machine comes
   to you.
  - Not Jira. It doesn't manage tickets. It reads Jira to understand what needs
  doing.
  - Not an IDE plugin. It orchestrates across all your tools, not just your
  editor.

  ---
  The v6 design and where it fits

  Looking at the v6 prototype — the Feed (home), Browse (SOP filesystem),
  Editor, and Terminal — those are the four surfaces of the web UI (the Finch
  interface). The terminal on the right panel is the Root interface embedded in
  the web. The feed cards with approval buttons are The Machine reaching out
  when it has something for you.

  The pieces are there. What was missing was the mental model of why: The
  Machine is always watching, always learning, reaches out when relevant,
  executes when trusted. The UI is just the window into it.

✻ Cogitated for 1m 28s

❯ this srticulation is very helpful. I am guessing this is like building        
  anthropic or openai. All the pieces needs to have a certain maturity and      
  feature before they become impactful.                                         

⏺ The analogy is right in one way and misleading in another. Worth being precise
   about both.

  ---
  Where it's like Anthropic/OpenAI

  The maturity curve is real. The Machine isn't impressive until several things
  work together:

  - SOPs exist (org knowledge encoded)
  - Agent loop executes them reliably
  - Integrations see your tools (GitHub, Jira, Slack)
  - Trust is calibrated (knows what to auto-approve vs ask)
  - Background mode watches and initiates

  None of these alone is the product. Like a model that can reason but has no
  training data, or training data with no model — individually they're inert.
  Together they're the thing.

  ---
  Where it's different from Anthropic/OpenAI

  Anthropic needed to train a frontier model before anything worked. That's
  years of research and hundreds of millions in compute before a single useful
  product exists. The "rocket before launch" problem.

  kloudi is different. The LLM already exists. You're not building the
  intelligence — you're building the organizational layer on top of it. That
  means:

  You can be useful on day one with one SOP, one engineer, one integration.

  Day 1 useful:
    kloudi (CLI) + engineering/impl SOP + Jira integration

    > kloudi
    ACME-456 assigned to you. I have a plan. Run it?
    > yes
    [reads ticket] [reads ERD] [writes code] [creates PR]
    Done.

  That's it. No web UI. No background mode. No feed. No onboarding mode. No
  learning. Just: machine reads a ticket, assembles context, writes code,
  creates a PR. One SOP working reliably for one engineer.

  That's not what Anthropic could ship before GPT-3 was trained. But it's what
  kloudi can ship before The Machine is complete.

  ---
  The maturity curve

  STAGE 0 — One engineer, one SOP, one integration
    Value: saves you 30min per ticket
    Needed: CLI + execution engine + Jira/GitHub MCP + one SOP
    Who uses it: you (Nitish)

  STAGE 1 — Your team, core SOPs, basic trust
    Value: team runs SOPs consistently, quality compounds
    Needed: web UI (feed + approvals) + auth + 5-10 SOPs + trust gates
    Who uses it: flywl engineering team

  STAGE 2 — The Machine emerges
    Value: proactive, background, initiates without being asked
    Needed: observer (webhooks) + background mode + morning briefing
    Who uses it: whole org notices something different is happening

  STAGE 3 — Self-improving
    Value: The Machine gets better at your org over time
    Needed: learning mode + trace analysis + SOP improvement proposals
    Who uses it: org feels like it has institutional memory

  STAGE 4 — Other orgs
    Value: onboarding mode reverse-engineers any new org
    Needed: onboarding mode + evaluation engine + quality thresholds
    Who uses it: customers

  The Anthropic equivalent would be: they needed Stage 4 (frontier model) before
   Stage 0 (any product). You have the inverse — Stage 0 is achievable in weeks
  and already valuable.

  ---
  The actual moat

  What you're building that can't be bought or replicated easily:

  The AI-native execution layer. Every other piece has substitutes:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │         Piece         │     Substitute      │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Canvas / graph editor │ React Flow + n8n UX │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Storage               │ Postgres + S3       │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Auth                  │ Better Auth         │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Integrations          │ MCP tools           │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Terminal UI           │ xterm.js            │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Scheduling            │ Render cron         │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  But the combination of: SOP graph as context assembly instructions + LLM as 
  executor + execution traces that improve the SOPs — that's not available
  anywhere. That's what Process Street, Notion, n8n, and Temporal each miss.
  None of them have the AI-native learning loop.

  So the build strategy is: adopt everything around the moat, build the moat.

  ---
  What this means for starting now

  You don't need all six parallel sessions from the prompt to complete before
  you're useful. You need:

  1. One working SOP end-to-end — engineering/impl or engineering/review-pr.
  Real Jira ticket in → real PR out. Everything else is scaffolding until this
  works.
  2. You using it daily — Stage 0 is you as the operative. The Machine's first
  mission is making your own work faster. If it doesn't save you 30 minutes a
  day, nothing else matters.
  3. Then: one teammate — the moment a second person uses it and you don't have
  to explain anything (The Machine does the onboarding), you've crossed from
  tool to platform.

  The six parallel sessions are the right architecture work. But the
  implementation sequence should start at Stage 0 and validate before building
  toward Stage 4. Build the paper airplane first. The rocket is the right
  destination — just don't wait for it before flying.

⏺ Here's the mental map, saved to docs/product-architecture.md. Let me walk you
  through what it says.

  ---
  The two views you asked for

  Breadth first — every user, every scenario, every module is mapped. The
  journey map covers 8 stages: setup, morning workflow, core work, background
  mode, SOP authoring, team workflows, new hire onboarding, and learning. Four
  personas: Root (engineer at CLI), Finch (PM on web), Admin (configures the
  org), and the Machine itself operating autonomously.

  Depth first — every module has 4 maturity levels (L1 = shippable, L4 = fully
  autonomous). The execution engine alone has 4 distinct levels from "single SOP
   runs" to "self-corrects and predicts failures." You don't need all four
  levels of all modules to ship. You need L1 across a small number of modules.

  ---
  The architecture answer at $100M

  9 independent services across 4 layers — product surfaces, intelligence,
  knowledge, and platform. Each team owns their services, their data, and their
  deploy contract. The key insight is the dependency order:

  Platform → Knowledge → Intelligence → Trust → Brain → Product surfaces

  You can't build Intelligence before Platform. You can't build Brain before
  Intelligence. But Integrations and Brain can run in parallel once Intelligence
   is unblocked.

  47 engineers across 9 teams. At $200k fully-loaded average that's ~$9M/year on
   headcount, leaving $91M for infrastructure, go-to-market, and runway. Very
  healthy.

  ---
  The Stage 1 cut

  Three engineers and a designer, 8 weeks. Just Platform + Intelligence at L1 
  depth. Five SOPs that prove the product:

  1. engineering/impl — ticket in → PR out
  2. engineering/review-pr — PR opened → review comment
  3. engineering/write-erd — PRD → ERD
  4. product/draft-prd — problem → PRD
  5. engineering/run-postmortem — incident → doc

  If those five work reliably, the product thesis is proven. Everything else is
  building toward the full Machine.

  ---
  The one decision that gates everything

  The AI-native execution engine (inner loop). Everything downstream — context
  assembly, backlinks, learning, onboarding — depends on that working at L1.
  That's the first thing the Intelligence team ships. It's the paper airplane.
  The rest is building the rocket.

  What's completely missing from the sessions:
  - Trust Service (flagged as "not designed yet" everywhere, still true)
  - Observer + Planner (Brain layer, but only onboarding has a session)
  - Feed Service (mentioned everywhere, no design session)
  - Integration Service / Tool Registry (no session)
  - CLI design (no session, just assumed in E)
  - Knowledge Graph + backlinks (touched in A but no dedicated session)

  The sessions were the right questions to ask in April. The architecture doc
  changes the frame.

  ---
  kloudi-fs doesn't make sense right now

  The idea is: build an open-source agent filesystem, donate it to vercel-labs
  or Linux Foundation AAIF.

  Three reasons it's the wrong move at this stage:

  1. It's not the moat. The moat is the AI-native execution layer +
  organizational knowledge network. The filesystem is plumbing. Mesa already
  does this. Git already does versioning. Building a new agent filesystem
  infrastructure competes for the same engineering time as building The Machine.

  2. Wrong sequencing. You'd be building kloudi-fs based on what you think
  agents need in a filesystem. The right time to build it is after you've run
  The Machine in production for a year and know exactly what primitives were
  missing. Extract from experience, don't predict from theory.

  3. Two products = two problems. Open source projects need maintenance,
  community, RFCs, documentation. That's a real management tax on a team that
  should be building The Machine. Session G itself says "new repo, separate
  community, donation strategy, Linux Foundation approach" — that's months of
  work before a line of production code ships.

  Use Mesa for Phase 1-3. If Mesa proves insufficient, you have the $100M to
  build kloudi-fs properly at that point, with real production requirements
  behind it.

  ---
  What to actually do now

  You said you want to start building so you can use this day-to-day. You have
  enough design to start Stage 1 today. The sessions that matter right now are:

  Keep:
  - Session C (DB schema) — but scoped only to Stage 1 modules, not all 5
  layers. Takes 2 hours. Unblocks implementation.
  - Session E (implementation) — but scoped to Stage 1 only: PR #7 fixes +
  Better Auth + execution engine AI-native + 5 working SOPs + CLI + basic feed.

  Defer until Stage 2:
  - Session B (Editor framework — not in Stage 1)
  - Session D (Onboarding mode — not in Stage 1)
  - Session G (kloudi-fs — use Mesa)

  New sessions needed, but not before Stage 1:
  - Trust model design (critical for Stage 2, not Stage 1)
  - Observer + Planner design (Brain layer, Stage 2-3)

  The architecture doc gives you the full map. Stage 1 is a small box in the
  bottom-left of that map. You don't need to design the whole map before
  building that box.

  The two things blocking Stage 1:
  1. Session C (quick — 2 hours, just Stage 1 schema)
  2. Then Session E (implementation)

  Everything else can wait until the paper airplane is flying.

❯ where will ai native executuion engine fit here                               

⏺ It's not one service among many. It's the nucleus everything else is organized
   around. Let me redraw the architecture with that clarity.

                      ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                      │  WHAT FEEDS INTO THE ENGINE                 │
                      │                                             │
           SOPs ──────┤  Knowledge Layer                           │
       (graph JSON)   │  · SOP Service: loads the graph            │
                      │  · Backlink resolver: fetches prior         │
                      │    SOP outputs as context                   │
                      │  · Artifact Service: PRDs, ERDs as context  │
                      │                                             │
        Integrations ─┤  Tool Registry                             │
    (GitHub, Jira...) │  · MCP tools available at each step        │
                      │  · Builtin / stdio / HTTP routing           │
                      │                                             │
           Trust ─────┤  Trust Service                             │
        (per-user)    │  · Inspector pipeline per tool call        │
                      └──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         │
                                         ▼
                      ╔══════════════════════════════════════════╗
                      ║                                          ║
                      ║      AI-NATIVE EXECUTION ENGINE          ║
                      ║           (the inner loop)               ║
                      ║                                          ║
                      ║  1. Load SOP graph                       ║
                      ║  2. For each node:                       ║
                      ║     - assemble context from sources      ║
                      ║     - LLM turn (context + instruction)   ║
                      ║     - tool calls → inspectors → execute  ║
                      ║     - trust gate? → pause + resume       ║
                      ║     - advance to next node               ║
                      ║  3. SOP complete                         ║
                      ║                                          ║
                      ║  LLM IS the control flow.                ║
                      ║  Graph IS the context assembly path.     ║
                      ║  No if/else on node types.               ║
                      ║                                          ║
                      ╚══════════════════════════════════════════╝
                                         │
                      ┌──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┐
                      │  WHAT READS FROM THE ENGINE                 │
                      │                                             │
                      ├── EventStream → WebSocket → CLI / Web feed  │
                      │   (every token, every tool call, every gate)│
                      │                                             │
                      ├── Trace writer → Postgres ExecutionNode     │
                      │   (every node: input, output, tokens, time) │
                      │                                             │
                      ├── Learning Engine ← reads traces            │
                      │   (quality scoring, pattern detection)      │
                      │                                             │
                      └── Feed Service ← consumes completion events │
                          (creates feed cards, approval buttons)    │
                          ─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Then one layer up, wrapping the engine:

                      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
                      │  AGENT CORE (the outer loop)             │
                      │                                          │
                      │  · Holds the conversation                │
                      │  · Identifies which SOP to run           │
                      │  · Calls Execution Engine as a tool      │
                      │  · Handles steering messages             │
                      │  · Routes output: terminal or feed       │
                      └──────────────────────────────────────────┘

          Then one layer further out:

                      ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
                      │  OBSERVER + PLANNER (the brain)          │
                      │                                          │
                      │  · Webhook fires (GitHub, Jira, Slack)   │
                      │  · Planner identifies relevant SOP       │
                      │  · Hands to Agent Core                   │
                      │  · Agent Core calls Execution Engine     │
                      └──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ---
  Where it sits in Stage 1 vs later stages

  STAGE 1      Execution Engine (AI-native inner loop)
               ↑ the only thing that matters
               Everything else in Stage 1 exists to call it
               and show its output

  STAGE 2      Agent Core (outer loop) wraps it
               Observer triggers it without user prompt
               Backlink resolver makes context assembly real

  STAGE 3      Learning Engine reads its traces
               Planner predicts what it should run next
               Morning briefing = Planner → Agent Core → Engine

  STAGE 4      Onboarding runs it to generate SOPs
               Enterprise sandboxing isolates it per org

  ---
  The one-sentence answer

  Every other service in the architecture either feeds data into the execution 
  engine, or reads events out of it. It's not in the Intelligence Layer — it IS 
  the Intelligence Layer. Everything else is input or output.

  This is also why Session A (agent loop design) had to happen before Session C
  (DB schema). The schema is shaped by what the engine needs to persist —
  iteration budget, context window state, trust decisions, node outputs. You
  can't design the tables without knowing what the engine does.

  On the archive principle: the right test isn't "is this old?" — it's "does
  reading this today help or confuse?" A 2020 decision that's still the
  foundation of the architecture belongs in current. A yesterday decision that's
   been reversed belongs in trash.

  right analogy. I replaced the strategy with a convenience shortcut.

⏺ Yes, this is needed — and it's a different kind of doc than anything that
  exists. Let me explain why before writing it.

  The architecture docs describe what to build. The design doc describes how it
  looks. Neither answers: what are the things in the system, what do they mean 
  to a user, and how do they compose into value?

  Without this, two problems happen as you build v1:
  1. Features get built in isolation — a developer builds "trust gates" without
  understanding that Trust is an entity that shows up in 6 different user
  moments
  2. User interviews produce unstructured feedback — someone says "I want to see
   what my team did this week" and without a shared vocabulary you build 3
  features when it's really one entity (Feed) in multiple contexts

  The entities you named from the PoI mapping are exactly right. Let me add the
  full set and write this as a living document — it updates after every user
  interview.

⏺ Yes, this was needed — and here's precisely why.

  The architecture docs tell you what to build. The domain model tells you what 
  things mean to users. Without it, two failure modes happen as you build v1 and
   run interviews:

  During building: An engineer building Trust gates builds them as a technical
  check before tool execution. Without the domain model, they don't see that
  Trust is also the entity that posts to the Feed (when in background mode),
  evolves over time per person, and is the mechanism by which the Machine earns
  autonomy. They build a check, not an entity.

  During interviews: Someone says "I want to know what my Machine did while I
  was in meetings." Without the domain model, you don't know whether that's a
  Feed problem, a Trace problem, an Execution analytics problem, or a new entity
   entirely. With it, you can map the answer immediately and know what to
  update.

  The doc covers 9 entities — The Machine, SOP, People, Tools, Execution, Trust,
   Trace, Feed, Organization — with JTBD, connections, how they compose into
  workflows, and critically: what changes with user interviews and a version
  history to track how the model evolves. Every interview should update this
  doc.

⏺ docs/current/ is now complete. Here's what's in it and what each file does:

  docs/current/
    00-start-here.md        ← press start. reading order, current status
    domain-model.md         ← the nouns. what everything IS and connects to
    v1-features.md          ← what to BUILD. every feature, JTBD, done-criteria
    the-machine-design.md   ← the locked architecture
    agent-loop-design.md    ← the AI-native execution engine
    product-architecture.md ← all modules, teams, Stage 1 cut
    session-summary.md      ← 12-hour design session context
    designs/
      design-preview-v6.html ← open in browser for UI reference

  The three documents form a complete product brief:
  1. domain-model.md — what the entities are and mean (the WHY)
  2. v1-features.md — what to build in v1 (the WHAT, with JTBDs)
  3. the-machine-design.md + agent-loop-design.md — how to build it (the HOW)

  The V1 features doc also doubles as your user interview guide — each interview
   question maps directly back to a JTBD, so every conversation either validates
   an assumption or tells you what to update in the domain model. That feedback
  loop — interview → update domain model → refine features — is how the docs
  stay accurate as you build.

❯ stepping back I understand where agent loop will go, where ui will go but     
  where will a virtualfs will go, where will a terminal go how will sandbox it  
  for background agents that is not clear to me. are we even doing that right   
  now?                                                                          

⏺ Good question to pause on. Let me draw exactly where each piece fits and
  answer the "are we doing this now" directly.

  ---
  V1 architecture — what's actually running

  V1 is simpler than the full design. Interactive only. Terminal always open.
  One engineer.

  ENGINEER'S MACHINE                    SERVER (Render)
  ─────────────────────                 ────────────────────
  ~/.kloudi/orgs/          ←reads
    guides/
    projects/

  kloudi CLI               ←────────── WebSocket ──────────→  Execution Engine
  (kloudi run, init, ls)               (EventStream: tokens,       (inner loop)
                                        trust gates, node events)       │
                                                                         │
  Filesystem tools                                                  LLM Gateway
  (read_file, write_file,                                          (Claude API)
   bash, git)                                                            │
       │                                                            Tool
  Registry
       └─── called by agent ──────────────────────────────────────── (MCP tools)
            via CLI executor                                         GitHub MCP
                                                                     Jira MCP

                                        Postgres (Neon)
                                        (Sop, Execution,
                                         ExecutionNode, Org)

  That's it for V1. No kloudi-fs. No web terminal. No sandbox. No background
  mode.

  ---
  Where each piece fits — and when

  Virtual filesystem (kloudi-fs)

  V1: Not needed.

  For V1, SOPs are markdown files in ~/.kloudi/orgs/ on your local machine. The
  CLI reads them directly. The execution engine reads the Sop.graph jsonb copy
  from Postgres (already synced when you kloudi init). Nothing needs a virtual
  filesystem.

  kloudi-fs becomes necessary at V2 when:
  - Multiple engineers need their local ~/.kloudi/ to stay in sync
  - The web UI needs to read SOP markdown (not just metadata from Postgres)
  - Background mode needs the server to read SOP files without a local terminal
  open

  V1: local files + Postgres jsonb copy = sufficient
  V2: kloudi-fs as the sync layer between local and cloud

  Terminal (wterm)

  V1: CLI only. Web terminal is V2.

  The CLI (kloudi run) already works — it renders trust gates, streams output,
  handles trust gate prompts. That IS the terminal for V1.

  wterm (the embedded web terminal in the right panel) comes at V2 when the web
  UI needs a terminal that connects to the agent loop on the server. Right now
  the web only needs the feed (approval cards, execution history) — not a full
  terminal.

  V1: kloudi CLI = the terminal
  V2: wterm in web right panel connects to same agent loop via WebSocket

  Background agent sandbox

  V1: Not in scope at all. Background mode is V2.

  V1 is interactive only — the engineer is always at the terminal. The moment
  they close it, The Machine stops. That's fine for V1.

  When background mode arrives (V2), there are two kinds of background tasks:

  LIGHT background tasks (API tools only):
    review-pr, post-comment, update-jira-ticket
    → Server calls GitHub/Jira via MCP HTTP directly
    → No sandbox needed — no local tools required
    → Server can do this today

  HEAVY background tasks (local tools needed):
    engineering/impl in background — needs git, write_file, run tests
    → Server can't call these (no local machine)
    → Needs a cloud sandbox: Modal / Daytona / Render job
    → This is V3 problem

  So the progression is:

  V1: Interactive only. Terminal open. No background.
  V2: Light background (API tools). Observer + webhooks.
      Server calls GitHub/Jira directly. No sandbox.
  V3: Heavy background (local tools). Cloud sandbox.
      Server spins up an isolated environment per execution.

  ---
  What this means for the architecture diagram

  The product-architecture.md shows the full picture at $100M scale. Here's how
  to read it against V1:

  SERVICE                     V1?     V2?     V3?
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────
  Auth Service                ✓
  SOP Service                 ✓
  Execution Engine            ✓
  LLM Gateway                 ✓
  Tool Registry               ✓ (GitHub + Jira only)
  Trust Service               ✓ (basic gates only)
  Feed Service                ✓ (basic cards)
  API Gateway                 ✓
  CLI                         ✓
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────
  kloudi-fs / Mesa            -       ✓
  wterm (web terminal)        -       ✓
  Observer Service            -       ✓
  Planner Service             -       ✓
  Notification Service        -       ✓
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────
  Cloud Sandbox               -       -       ✓
  Learning Engine             -       -       ✓
  Onboarding Engine           -       -       ✓

  ---
  The short answer

  You're right to question it. For V1:
  - kloudi-fs: build in parallel (separate repo), but kloudi.os V1 doesn't
  depend on it. Postgres + local files is enough.
  - wterm: not needed. CLI handles the terminal. Web only needs feed.
  - Sandbox: nowhere near V1. V2 light background needs no sandbox. V3 heavy
  background does.

  V1 is the CLI talking to the server talking to Claude talking to GitHub/Jira.
  Everything else in the architecture is real and coming — just not now.

  ---
  Where each piece fits in V1

  Virtual filesystem (kloudi-fs): Not needed for V1.
  SOPs live in ~/.kloudi/ locally and sync via Git for multi-user. Git
  clone/pull is sufficient when you have a small team. kloudi-fs (real-time
  sync, no git commands) is V2 when teams hit friction with the git workflow.

  Web terminal (wterm): Yes, V1.
  The right panel in the web UI is wterm connecting to the same agent loop as
  the CLI via WebSocket. Engineer can run SOPs, watch streaming output, and
  approve trust gates from the browser. Same experience as the CLI, different
  surface.

  Background agent sandbox: Split decision.

  LIGHT background (V1) — API tools only, no sandbox needed:
    PR opened → review-pr SOP runs on server
    Server calls GitHub MCP HTTP to read PR, post comment
    No local filesystem access. No sandbox. Works today.

  HEAVY background (V2) — local tools needed, sandbox required:
    engineering/impl in background → needs git, write_file, run tests
    Server can't call local tools without a machine
    → Two options:
      a) Persistent CLI as background process on engineer's machine
      b) Cloud sandbox (Modal/Daytona) per execution
    → This is V2 decision, not V1

  ---
  What this adds to V1 vs V0

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬───────────────┬────────────┐

  ├──────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Observer (GitHub/Jira webhooks)  │ —             │ ✓          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Planner (event → SOP)            │ —             │ ✓          │

⏺ You're pointing at something that's subtly wrong in the current framing. Let

⏺ Found the exact problem. Line 72: "Surfaces The Machine operates through" —
  this frames CLI as a surface the Machine pushes through. It's backwards.

  The correct model:

  MACHINE INITIATES (Machine → Human):
    Observes PR opened → posts to Feed → Slack ping
    Sees ticket assigned → assembles plan → Feed: "ready when you are"
    Morning arrives → brief posted to Feed → Slack

    The Machine doesn't wait. It reaches out.

  HUMAN INITIATES (Human → Machine conversation):
    Engineer opens: kloudi  (CLI)
    PM opens: web terminal (wterm)

    These are PORTALS the human uses to converse with The Machine.
    The Machine was already running before you opened the terminal.
    It just wasn't talking to you directly yet.

  The Machine doesn't live in the terminal. The terminal is a phone you pick up
  to call it. The Feed and Slack are how it calls you.

  The key distinction that was wrong everywhere: the old framing said "surfaces
  The Machine operates through" implying the Machine pushes through all four.
  The correct model is:

  when the human opens them — but The Machine was already running and working

❯ why are we calling  **Process SOP** (`guides/`)  as guides. Is this the       
  common lingo. What do open organisation like gitlab call their company        
  handbook and what does organisation like linear, airbnb strip call their      
  internal proce                                                                

⏺ Good question. Let me think through what real companies actually call this,
  then give a recommendation.

  ---
  What major companies call their internal processes

  GitLab — "The Handbook." handbook.gitlab.com. Public, comprehensive, covers
  engineering to finance to CEO shadow. Every internal process is a handbook
  entry. The word they use: handbook.

  Valve — "Handbook for New Employees." Famous public doc. One word: handbook.

  Basecamp/37signals — "Employee Handbook" + "Shape Up" for product process.
  Also handbook.

  Linear — "The Linear Method." A public principles doc on how they build
  product. Not structured as folders — more of a manifesto. They'd call
  individual entries principles or methods.

  Stripe — Engineering uses runbooks (operational: how to deploy, how to handle
  incidents). Product uses playbooks. No single term for everything.

  Airbnb — Engineering: runbooks for operational procedures. Broader: playbooks
  for repeatable processes.

  SRE/DevOps generally — runbooks for anything operational. "Runbook: deploy to
  production." "Runbook: database migration."

  Sales/GTM teams — playbooks universally. "Sales playbook." "Onboarding
  playbook."

  ---
  The problem with "guides/"

  "Guides" has an educational/tutorial connotation. You write a guide for
  someone learning something — a "Getting Started Guide," a "Developer Guide."
  That's documentation, not process.

  What we actually have in guides/ is: how engineers review PRs, how PMs write
  PRDs, how the team runs a postmortem. These aren't guides for learners —
  they're operational procedures the team runs repeatedly.

  ---
  The right term for kloudi

  Three candidates:

  playbooks/ — Most natural in modern tech companies across all departments. Has
   an operational feel. Not department-specific. "What's the playbook for this?"
   is a sentence real people say.

  handbook/ — What GitLab made famous. Authoritative, comprehensive. But feels
  like one document, not a folder of many. Also has a company culture
  connotation beyond just processes.

  runbooks/ — Too engineering/DevOps specific. A PM would never call their PRD
  process a "runbook."

  My recommendation: playbooks/

  But there's a deeper question: should kloudi enforce the folder name at all?

  GitLab calls it handbook. Your org might call it playbooks. Another org might
  call it how-we-work. The filesystem structure could let orgs name it
  themselves — kloudi's convention is a default, not a mandate.

  For now: rename guides/ → playbooks/ in all docs. It's the most universally
  understood term for "how we do things repeatably" across engineering, product,
   and leadership. Update it if user interviews show orgs already have a name
  they prefer.

  Should I update all docs — filesystem structure in the-machine-design.md,
  domain-model.md, v1-features.md, product-architecture.md — to use playbooks/
  instead of guides/?

✻ Baked for 51s

❯ if everything is a folder - we can recommend a starting point but the         
  question is should people have the ability to change top level nouns. Its     
  like saying we can go and rename the documents folder in mac. is that         
  possible?                                                                     

⏺ This is an OS design decision and the Mac analogy is exactly right. Let me
  think through it clearly.

  ---
  The Mac precedent

  On macOS:
  - Documents, Downloads, Desktop — you CAN rename them, but apps that hardcode
  ~/Documents/ break
  - Library, Applications, System — you cannot rename these at all. The OS
  treats them as contracts.
  - At first setup, when you name your user account, your home folder gets that
  name — that's a one-time config decision

  The question for kloudi: which category do playbooks/ and projects/ fall into?

  ---
  The answer: they're system folders, not user folders

  The top-level folders aren't arbitrary labels. They're contracts the Machine
  depends on:

  - The Machine looks in playbooks/ to find process SOPs to execute
  - Backlinks reference playbooks/engineering/impl.md — rename the folder and
  every backlink breaks
  - Cross-org SOP sharing (V5) requires a common schema — if GitLab has
  handbook/ and you have playbooks/, sharing breaks
  - The graph JSON in Postgres stores paths. Rename the folder, the execution
  engine can't find the SOP.

  So renaming mid-flight is like renaming ~/Library on macOS — technically
  possible, breaks everything.

  ---
  The right model: configure once at org init, fixed after

  kloudi init
    → "What do you call your process documentation?"
    → Options: playbooks / handbook / runbooks / custom
    → Stored in .kloudi/config.json as the canonical name
    → Machine uses this name everywhere: paths, backlinks, UI labels

  After init: fixed. Same as naming your Mac at setup.

  This handles the GitLab case: they onboard with handbook/ and The Machine maps
   their existing handbook/engineering/ structure directly. No forced rename.

  It also handles the default case: most orgs starting fresh pick from the
  suggested options.

  ---
  What goes in config.json

  {
    "org": "flywl",
    "folders": {
      "playbooks": "handbook",     ← GitLab would set this
      "projects": "projects"        ← most orgs keep this
    }
  }

  The Machine reads this once at startup. Internally it always thinks in terms
  of playbooks — externally it shows whatever the org configured. Like macOS
  reading a localization file: the system calls it Library internally but shows
  Bibliothek in German.

  ---
  The starting default

  playbooks/ — it's the most universally understood term across engineering,
  product, and leadership in modern tech companies, and it has an operational
  feel (not educational like "guides"). GitLab would override it to handbook.
  Most new orgs won't change it.

  Should I update all docs to use playbooks/ as the default and add the
  config.json convention to the filesystem spec in the-machine-design.md?

❯ now let's look at **Artifact SOP** (`projects/`) — what we produce. Nouns.    
  PRDs, ERDs, specs, test plans                                                 
                                                                                
  nobody calls artifact as sops even though they are. also you only talk about  
  docs as artifacts but even code is a form of artifact sop? is that reasoning  
  correct?                                                                      

⏺ Both points are correct. Let me work through them.

  ---
  Point 1: Don't call them "Artifact SOPs"

  Nobody says "let me read the Artifact SOP for beacon." They say "let me read
  the PRD for beacon." The "SOP" framing was useful internally during design to
  unify the model — but it should never surface to users or appear in docs as a
  user-facing term.

  What they actually are: work products. Things the org produces. Each one has a
   name people already use:

  - PRD is a PRD
  - ERD is an ERD
  - Postmortem is a postmortem
  - Test plan is a test plan

  The unifying concept in kloudi is that they all live in projects/, they all
  have a defined structure (sections, not steps), and The Machine generates
  them. But we call them by their actual names, not "Artifact SOPs."

  "Artifact SOP" is an internal design concept — remove it from all user-facing 
  language.

  ---
  Point 2: Code is an artifact too — and it's the most important one

  Yes. This is the point. Look at the full chain:

  PLAYBOOKS (processes)     →    WORK PRODUCTS (what gets made)
  ───────────────────────        ──────────────────────────────

  product/draft-prd         →    projects/beacon/prd/v2.md        (document)
  engineering/write-erd     →    projects/beacon/erd/schema.md     (document)
  engineering/impl          →    github.com/flywl/service/PR #48   (code)
  engineering/review-pr     →    github.com/flywl/service/#42 comment (code)
  engineering/db-migrate    →    migration_20260503.sql             (code)
  engineering/qa            →    projects/beacon/qa/test-plan.md   (document)
                                 + test files in repo               (code)

  Every playbook produces something. Sometimes that something is a document.
  Sometimes it's code. Sometimes it's both. The distinction is just where it 
  lives — not what it is.

  Document work products  → projects/  (in kloudi filesystem)
  Code work products      → repos      (in GitHub, pointed to by repo.json)

  Both are artifacts of processes. Both are tracked. Both matter.

  The PRD informs the code. The ERD shapes the schema. The postmortem produces
  action items that become tickets that produce code. The whole chain is
  documents and code alternating — and The Machine produces things at every
  step.

  ---
  What this means for the model

  The current framing in domain-model.md:

  ▎ Process SOP (playbooks/) — how we do things. Verbs.
  ▎ Artifact SOP (projects/) — what we produce. Nouns.

  Should become:

  ▎ Playbooks (playbooks/) — processes. How the org does things.
  ▎ Work products — what the org produces. Two kinds:
  ▎ - Documents (projects/) — PRDs, ERDs, specs, postmortems. Stored in kloudi 
  ▎ filesystem.
  ▎ - Code (repos) — PRs, migrations, tests, deployments. Stored in GitHub, 
  ▎ linked via repo.json.

  The Machine produces both. Traces record both. Trust gates apply to both
  (writing a file and creating a PR both need approval).

  ---
  Should I update domain-model.md and everywhere else "Artifact SOP" appears to
  use "work products" + the document/code split?

✻ Sautéed for 44s
