   solo entrepreneur using kloudi machine as well as teams of 100,000 engineers 
   with multiple products and multiple business units. we can start anywhere in 
   the spectrum and say we support till here but we should know how will we     
  keep extending the depth of the structure. also there is another spectrum I   
  want you to be conizant of - we are currently focussed on epd departments of  
  an org. but what stops us to bringing sales departments here or hr department 
   here that might be a different spectrum but I just want to see if you feel   
  this machine is extendable to them as well or not                             

⏺ Yes to both spectrums. And they're connected. Let me think through both
  carefully before touching the docs.

  ---
  Spectrum 1 — Organization size

  The same filesystem, same Machine, same model — just deeper:

  SOLO (1 person)
    playbooks/
      writing/        how I write content
      building/       how I ship features
      client/         how I onboard a client
    projects/
      newsletter/
      saas-product/

    Human IS Root, Finch, and Admin.
    Trust gates are reminders to yourself, not approvals.
    The Machine is a personal intelligence layer.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────

  SMALL TEAM (5–50)
    playbooks/
      engineering/
      product/
    projects/
      beacon/

    Cross-person trust gates now matter.
    One level deep. One product.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────

  COMPANY (50–500)
    playbooks/
      engineering/
      product/
      design/
      leadership/
    projects/
      beacon/
      platform/

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────

  ENTERPRISE (5,000–100,000+)
    business-units/
      azure/
        playbooks/
          engineering/
          product/
        projects/
          kubernetes/
      office/
        playbooks/
          engineering/
          product/
        projects/
          word/
    shared/            ← cross-BU playbooks: security, legal, compliance
      playbooks/
        security/
        legal/

  The structure extends by adding one level above the current depth. Solo has no
   hierarchy. Team has departments. Company has departments with sub-teams.
  Enterprise adds business units above that. Each level inherits from above and
  overrides locally — like DNS or CSS specificity.

  The Machine's architecture doesn't change at any level. The engine runs the
  same way whether it's Nitish running kloudi alone or an Azure team of 10,000
  engineers.

  ---
  Spectrum 2 — Beyond EPD

  The Machine is fully extensible to any department. Here's why the architecture
   is general:

  Every department in every org has:

  PROCESSES       WORK PRODUCTS       TOOLS              EVENTS
  ─────────────   ─────────────────   ────────────────   ────────────────────
  how to hire     job descriptions    Greenhouse/Lever   candidate applied
  how to close    proposals           Salesforce         deal moved to close
  how to invoice  invoices            QuickBooks         month-end arrived
  how to review   legal memos         DocuSign           contract received

  The Machine doesn't care which department. It watches tools, identifies the
  relevant playbook, assembles context, executes, pauses at trust gates. The
  primitives are universal.

  EPD is the wedge, not the ceiling:

  WAVE 1 (wedge)     Engineering, Product, Design
                     GitHub, Jira, Figma
                     Engineers adopt first — most comfortable with AI tools
                     Most deterministic inputs/outputs

  WAVE 2 (adjacent)  Data, DevOps, Security
                     Similar tool stack to EPD
                     Same culture

  WAVE 3 (business)  Sales, Marketing, Customer Success
                     Salesforce, HubSpot, Intercom
                     More human-judgment trust gates

  WAVE 4 (support)   HR, Finance, Legal, Operations
                     Greenhouse, Workday, QuickBooks, DocuSign
                     Higher compliance — stricter trust gates
                     More sensitive data

  WAVE 5 (full org)  Every department unified
                     Cross-department playbooks
                     Machine knows the whole company

  The only thing that changes between waves is the integrations and the playbook
   library. The engine, trust model, feed, terminal — identical.

  ---
  Code as work product — where it fits in the full picture

  Coming back to your original point: code isn't special. It's just the
  engineering department's version of what every department produces.

  DEPARTMENT     WORK PRODUCTS
  ────────────   ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Engineering    Code (PRs, migrations, tests, deployments) → repos
  Product        Documents (PRDs, roadmaps, specs) → projects/
  Design         Designs (mockups, design systems) → projects/ + Figma
  Sales          Proposals, contracts, account plans → projects/
  HR             Job descriptions, offer letters, reviews → projects/
  Finance        Budget models, expense reports → projects/
  Legal          Contracts, NDAs, memos → projects/

  Every department produces two things:
  1. Structured documents — live in projects/, authored with Machine assistance
  2. Executables — live in their native system (repos for code, Salesforce for
  deals, Workday for offers)

  The Machine produces both. The Trace records both. Trust gates guard both. The
   difference is only storage location.

  ---
  What the structure needs to support this scaling

  For org size (adding depth):
  The config.json already handles folder naming. Add one more field: structure.
  Solo gets flat. Enterprise gets business-units/ level.

  {
    "org": "microsoft",
    "structure": "enterprise",
    "folders": {
      "playbooks": "playbooks",
      "projects": "projects",
      "business_units": "business-units",
      "shared": "shared"
    }
  }

  For department expansion (adding breadth):
  Nothing changes architecturally. Add integrations, add playbooks. The Machine
  runs whatever it finds. Onboarding Mode already handles this — it scans
  whatever tools the org uses and generates playbooks from them.

  Trust gates get stricter as sensitivity increases:
  Engineering: auto for most actions
  HR: prompt for anything involving a person's data
  Finance: prompt for anything involving money
  Legal: block on anything outgoing without explicit approval

  ---
  The one-sentence version for both spectrums

  The Machine is an organizational intelligence that works the same way whether 
  you're a solo founder or a 100,000-person enterprise, and whether your 
  department writes code or closes deals or hires people. Processes, work 
  products, people, tools, and trust are universal. Only the content changes.

  Now updating the docs to capture this — the domain model needs the two
  spectrums, the work products need the document/executable split, and the "EPD
  is the wedge" expansion path needs to be explicit.

⏺ Done. Four docs updated. Here's what's now captured:

  Two spectrums locked into the model:

  Solo founder using kloudi as a personal intelligence layer → 100,000-person
  enterprise with nested business units. The filesystem grows one level of depth
   per tier. The Machine architecture never changes.

  EPD wedge → Sales → HR → Finance → Legal → full org. Only the integrations and
   playbook content change per wave. The primitives (processes, work products,
  people, tools, trust) are universal to every department.

  "Artifact SOP" is gone. Work products are now correctly split:
  - Documents — live in projects/, every department produces them
  - Executables — live in their native system: code in repos, deals in
  Salesforce, records in Workday

  Code is not special — it's engineering's version of what every department
  produces. The Trace records both, trust gates guard both, The Machine produces
   both.

✻ Brewed for 2m 21s

❯ My final thing here for you to think is - when we are onboarding an org       
  should we start with a department (say epd) and then bring their tools under  
  one umbrella. and once we do that do we take a stab at organising them first  
  and then giving the user to move them around? if yes then repo will play a    
  critical role and same with playbooks                                         

⏺ Yes. And you've just described the correct onboarding sequence. Let me think
  through why each part matters.

  ---
  Department-first is right

  Starting with the whole org is overwhelming — too much data, too many
  stakeholders, quality threshold impossible to hit fast. Starting with EPD
  means:
  - One person installs it (the engineering lead or CTO)
  - Two tools to connect (GitHub + Jira)
  - Scope is bounded
  - Time to first value is days, not weeks
  - Once EPD is working, other departments see it and ask to join

  The org expands department by department. Each addition is its own
  mini-onboarding, not a second Big Bang.

  ---
  Repos are the source of playbooks — not separate from them

  This is the critical insight. When you scan repos, you're not reading code —
  you're reading the operational DNA the engineering team embedded there over 
  time without knowing it:

  WHAT'S IN A REPO              WHAT IT BECOMES IN kloudi
  ────────────────────          ──────────────────────────────────
  CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md    →    playbooks/engineering/impl.md
  .github/workflows/*.yml  →    playbooks/engineering/deploy.md
  PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md →    playbooks/engineering/review-pr.md
  CONTRIBUTING.md          →    playbooks/engineering/code-style.md
  docs/adr/*.md            →    architecture decisions → context for all SOPs
  schema.prisma / *.sql    →    projects/{product}/erd/schema.md
  README.md                →    projects/{product}/overview.md

  The repos don't just tell you what was built. They tell you how the team
  works. That's the playbook. kloudi extracts what's implicit and makes it
  explicit and executable.

  Same for Jira:
  Jira workflow states     →    understanding of how work flows (Backlog →
  Review → Done)
  Issue types + labels     →    how the team categorizes work
  Sprint history           →    delivery cadence + velocity
  Who reviews whose PRs    →    trust profiles (who has autonomy over what)

  The Machine combines GitHub + Jira to reconstruct the team's actual process —
  not what their wiki says, but what they actually do, evidenced by three years
  of commits and tickets.

  ---
  Auto-organize then adjust — the right mental model

  Not "here's a blank canvas." Instead: "here's what I found — does this look 
  right?"

  MACHINE PROPOSES:

    playbooks/
      engineering/
        deploy-to-prod.md          ← extracted from .github/workflows/deploy.yml
        review-pr.md               ← extracted from PR template + observed
  patterns
        db-migration.md            ← inferred from 23 migration files across
  repos
      product/
        (nothing found — connect Confluence to generate these)

    projects/
      beacon/                      ← your main product
        erd/
          schema-v2.md             ← extracted from schema.prisma
        repo.json                  ← points to fdx-service-pim,
  fdx-service-platform

    Missing: product/draft-prd, design processes, postmortem
    Confidence: engineering 70%, product 20%

    Move things around. Rename anything. I'll lock this structure when you say
  go.

  The user then interacts with this proposed structure — drag, rename, split,
  merge. Once they confirm, The Machine locks the config and starts operating
  from it.

  The quality threshold isn't "did we generate everything" — it's "is what we
  generated good enough to execute?" A generated playbook that Machine can
  actually run scores higher than a beautifully written one it can't.

  ---
  The sequencing

  ONBOARDING SEQUENCE:

  1. CONNECT (one department)
     → GitHub repos + Jira project
     → Optional: Confluence, Slack

  2. SCAN (Machine reads everything)
     → Repos: CLAUDE.md, CI/CD, PR templates, ADRs, schemas
     → Jira: workflow states, issue types, sprint history
     → Confluence: process docs, meeting notes (if connected)
     → Cross-reference: who commits what, who reviews whom

  3. EXTRACT
     → Machine generates playbook drafts from what it found
     → Identifies gaps: "product processes not found — need Confluence or human
  input"
     → Maps existing work to projects/ structure

  4. PROPOSE (Machine speaks first)
     → Shows proposed playbooks/ + projects/ structure
     → Marks confidence per playbook (high/medium/needs-human)
     → Highlights what's missing

  5. ADJUST (user moves things around)
     → Drag to rename/reorganize
     → Accept Machine's drafts or rewrite
     → Fill in gaps the Machine couldn't infer

  6. VALIDATE (quality threshold)
     → Machine runs dry-run on each playbook
     → Flags ones that would fail execution (missing context, broken tools)
     → Threshold: 70% of playbooks executable before going live
     → Below threshold: iterate

  7. LIVE
     → Config locked
     → Machine starts watching tools
     → Background mode activates
     → Morning brief appears next morning

  ---
  What repos and playbooks unlock together

  The reason repos are critical is that they contain both sides of the equation 
  simultaneously:

  - The process (how we deploy → CI/CD workflow →
  playbooks/engineering/deploy.md)
  - The artifact (what we built → schema.prisma → projects/beacon/erd/)

  Scanning one repo gives you both a playbook AND a work product. You don't need
   Confluence if the repo has CLAUDE.md. You don't need a separate ERD document
  if schema.prisma exists and has good comments.

  This also means the quality of existing repos directly determines the quality 
  of initial playbooks. A team that writes good README files and CLAUDE.md gets
  better Machine onboarding than a team that doesn't. This is a virtuous cycle —
   teams that already document their processes get a better Machine, which
  incentivizes documenting processes.

  precisely:

⏺ Done. The onboarding brief is now accurate to the thinking you just did.

  To your question directly: yes, the sequence is right. Department first → 
  connect tools → scan repos (which contain both process and artifact DNA) → 
  Machine proposes the structure → user adjusts → quality threshold → live.

  The insight that makes it click: repos are not just code. They're the team's
  process documentation embedded organically over years — CLAUDE.md, CI/CD, PR
  templates, ADRs. A team that writes these well gets a better Machine
  onboarding because the Machine is reading their actual behavior, not their
  aspirational wiki. That's why repos play a critical role — they're the ground
  truth of how engineering actually works, and playbooks are the explicit
  version of what was implicit in them.

❯ awesome! now coming back to v1 and reviewing the docs myself. something you

⏺ Here's my honest read. Two things: what deserves your deepest attention, and
  where my thinking shifted.

  ---
  What to focus on deeply

  docs/current/v1-features.md — read every line

  Specifically:

  1. The V1 success criteria. This is the contract. Every criterion should feel
  like something you'd demo to a first customer. The one that deserves the most
  scrutiny:

  ▎ PR opened on GitHub → Machine notices → review-pr runs in background → feed 
  ▎ card appears → PM approves comment → posted to GitHub

  This is the most complex flow in V1 — it chains Observer → Planner → Execution
   → Trust gate → Feed → Resume. It's also the flow that proves the product. If
  this feels too ambitious for V1, say so now. It's much cheaper to scope it out
   before building than after.

  2. The "not V1" list. Every item on that list will try to creep back in during
   building. Read it and confirm you agree with each exclusion. Disagreements
  now cost nothing. Disagreements at week 6 are expensive.

  3. The JTBD under each entity in domain-model.md. These are the hypotheses
  you'll test in user interviews. If any of them feel wrong based on what you
  already know about your users, change them before building. A wrong JTBD
  becomes a wrong feature.

  ---
  Where my opinion changed — what it means for V1

  1. V1 scope itself (biggest shift)
  I originally said Observer, Planner, background mode, and wterm were V2. You
  correctly said that's the product — without them you have a fancy run command,
   not The Machine. This is now correctly in V1 and I stand by it. But it means
  V1 is genuinely large. The V0 → V1 sequence is not optional — you cannot skip
  V0 and jump straight to V1.

  2. Morning brief is V1, not V2
  I had it as V2 (requires Planner fully working). It should be V1. When you
  open kloudi, The Machine speaks first. That IS the product experience. Keep
  it.

  3. Slack notifications — consider moving to V2
  I put Slack in V1 as "light notifications." My opinion has changed: for a team
   of 2-5 (your initial users), both the engineer and PM will be checking the
  web feed. Slack becomes important at scale, not at launch. Building a Slack
  app is non-trivial and diverts effort from the core flow. Move Slack to V2.
  The web feed is the payphone for V1.

  4. Agent switching in wterm — move to V2
  I included a dropdown to switch between The Machine, Claude Code, Codex in the
   web terminal. For V1, there's only one agent — The Machine. Agent switching
  is a marketplace feature. Remove it from V1. Add it when you have multiple
  agents to switch between.

  5. "Artifact SOP" is gone from the model
  Work products are now documents + executables. Code is an artifact. This
  doesn't change what you build in V1 — but it changes how you describe it when
  talking to users. Don't use "Artifact SOP" with anyone.

  ---
  The one honest tension in V1

  V1 is ambitious — Observer + Planner + background mode + wterm + Feed
  approvals + AI-native engine, all at once. That's 5 new things simultaneously
  on top of V0.

  The risk is not that it's wrong. It's that it ships late or half-finished. The
   discipline is: V0 must be done and working before a single line of V1 code is
   written. V0 is not a throwaway — it's the foundation V1 builds on. If the
  execution engine is flaky, everything V1 builds on top of it will be flaky
  too.

  After removing Slack and agent switching, V1 looks like this:

  V1 MUST-HAVE (keep):
    Observer + Planner (webhooks → SOPs)
    Background mode (API tools, no terminal)
    wterm in web right panel
    Feed approval cards
    Morning brief
    PM approves from web

  V1 REMOVE (defer to V2):
    Slack notifications
    Agent switching dropdown

  That's a cleaner V1 that still proves The Machine is proactive and operates on
   both surfaces.

※ recap: Built kloudi's complete product foundation today: mental model, domain model, V1 features with JTBDs, and organised all docs into current/future/archive/trash. Next action: fix the 4 P0/P1 bugs in TODOS.md so kloudi init and kloudi run actually work end to end.

❯ **Jobs to be done (Engineer):**                                               
  - When ACME-456 is assigned to me, I want the Machine to already have a plan  
  assembled so I don't spend 30 minutes gathering context.                      
  - When a trust gate fires, I want to see exactly what the Machine is about to 
   do and approve/reject in one action.                                         
                                                                                
  **Jobs to be done (PM/Manager):**                                             
  - When I open my laptop Monday morning, I want to see what the Machine did    
  over the weekend so I know what my team accomplished.                         
  - When the Machine proposes a plan, I want to approve or edit it without      
  needing a terminal.                                                           
                                                                                
  **Jobs to be done (Admin):**                                                  
  - When a new engineer joins, I want to configure their trust level so the     
  Machine knows what to ask vs auto-approve for them.                           
  - When we connect a new tool (Jira), I want the Machine to start watching it  
  immediately.                                                                  
                                                                                
                                                                                
  Here I think we need to think more don't you think?                           

⏺ Yes. The current JTBDs are thin in two specific ways — they capture the happy
  path narrow use case but miss the arc, the failure modes, and the emotional
  reality of each persona's relationship with The Machine. Let me think through
  each one more carefully.

  ---
  What's missing from the current JTBDs

  Three dimensions that aren't there:

  1. The arc — how does the relationship with The Machine evolve from day 1 to
  day 90? The first week an engineer is watching every trust gate nervously. By
  week 8 they're auto-approving most things. That shift matters and the JTBDs
  should capture both ends.
  2. When The Machine is wrong — every persona needs a JTBD for correction and
  override. Right now the JTBDs assume the Machine is always right. Real
  adoption lives or dies in the moments when it isn't.
  3. The social dimension — using The Machine affects how people relate to their
   teammates. The engineer whose PR was created by the Machine needs their
  teammates to understand that. The PM approving something needs confidence
  they're not rubber-stamping. These are invisible in the current JTBDs.

  ---
  Engineer (Root) — expanded

  GETTING STARTED:
  When I first try kloudi, I want to see it do one thing correctly so I
  trust it enough to give it something real to do. Not a demo — actual work.

  DAILY CONTEXT ASSEMBLY:
  When ACME-456 lands on my plate, I want the Machine to have already read
  the ticket, found the related ERD, read the relevant code — so I start
  with full context, not 30 minutes of hunting.

  STAYING IN FLOW:
  When I'm deep in implementation, I want the Machine to handle all the
  coordination work (update Jira, ping the reviewer, create the PR) so I
  never have to context-switch to a browser.

  TRUST GATE CONFIDENCE:
  When the Machine pauses for approval, I want to see enough context to
  make a fast, confident decision — not a nervous rubber stamp where I'm
  hoping it's right.

  REDIRECTING MID-EXECUTION:
  When the Machine has the wrong plan, I want to say "actually, do it
  this way instead" and have it adapt — not start over from scratch.

  WHEN IT GETS IT WRONG:
  When the Machine makes a mistake (wrong file, wrong approach), I want
  to correct it in a way it learns from — not just undo and move on.

  VISIBILITY WITH TEAMMATES:
  When the Machine creates a PR or posts a comment on my behalf, I want
  it clearly labeled as Machine-assisted so my team understands what
  happened without me having to explain it every time.

  END OF DAY:
  When I close my laptop, I want to know that everything is committed,
  pushed, ticketed, and updated — and the Machine handled the last 10%
  I always forget.

  ---
  PM / Manager (Finch) — expanded

  MORNING VISIBILITY:
  When I open my laptop, I want to know what the team shipped and what's
  blocked — without pinging anyone. Not a Jira dashboard. A briefing.

  APPROVING WITH CONFIDENCE:
  When the Machine needs a decision from me, I want enough context to
  approve confidently — not blindly clicking yes because I don't
  understand the technical detail.

  QUALITY GATES:
  When engineering is about to start on a feature, I want a clear moment
  where I sign off on the PRD/ERD — not find out the spec was wrong
  three sprints later.

  TRUSTING WHAT I CAN'T SEE:
  When the Machine worked overnight and I wasn't there, I want to be
  able to verify what it did without reading a trace log designed for
  engineers.

  PATTERN DETECTION:
  When the same kind of issue keeps happening (PRs always blocked for
  the same reason, same steps always failing), I want the Machine to
  surface it to me at the right level of abstraction so I can fix the
  process, not just the instance.

  WHEN THE MACHINE IS WRONG:
  When the Machine posts something incorrect on behalf of my team, I
  want to correct it and understand why it happened — before the
  external stakeholder sees it.

  ASYNC UNBLOCKING:
  When I'm in back-to-back meetings, I want the Machine to have handled
  everything it could and queued only the decisions that genuinely need me.

  ---
  Admin — this persona is the most underexplored

  The Admin JTBD barely exists in the current doc. But Admin is the person who
  decides if kloudi stays or gets cancelled. Their JTBDs are about control,
  safety, and org-level visibility:

  INITIAL TRUST:
  When I introduce kloudi to my team, I want to start with the Machine
  in a mode where it suggests but never acts autonomously — so my team
  builds trust before handing over control.

  TRUST CALIBRATION:
  When a new engineer joins, I want to set conservative trust levels
  that automatically evolve as they build a track record — so I'm not
  manually adjusting permissions forever.

  SOP GOVERNANCE:
  When a process needs to change (new deployment policy), I want to
  control who can edit that SOP and have changes go through review —
  the same way code goes through PRs.

  INTEGRATION HEALTH:
  When a connected tool has an issue (expired token, rate limit, webhook
  stopped firing), I want to know before the Machine silently fails or
  starts making bad decisions with stale data.

  AUDIT:
  When something goes wrong, I want a complete, human-readable record
  of exactly what the Machine did, why it did it, and who approved it.

  EXPANDING TO A NEW DEPARTMENT:
  When sales or HR wants to join kloudi, I want to add them without
  touching anything EPD has already set up — clean isolation.

  THE KILL SWITCH:
  When the Machine does something unexpected, I want to pause all
  autonomous action immediately and review what happened — without
  losing the state of in-progress work.

  ---
  The common thread across all three

  Every persona has a JTBD that's not in the current doc: "When the Machine is 
  wrong, I want to correct it in a way that makes it right the next time."

  This is actually one of the most important product moments — the correction
  loop. Get it wrong and users stop trusting The Machine. Get it right and trust
   compounds over time. It should be explicit in every persona's JTBD list and
  it drives design decisions: how do you surface what the Machine did, how do
  you make it easy to say "not this", how does that correction get learned?

  ---
  Should I rewrite the People section of domain-model.md with these expanded
  JTBDs? And do any of these surface a feature that's missing from
  v1-features.md — particularly the Admin kill switch and the "correction loop"?

✻ Baked for 1m 7s

❯ Before you rewrite think org design as well. Same example solopreneur vs      
  100,000 member org. a pm means different for different orgs and engineer      
  could be a consultant or a product engineer. how are we capturing that level  
  of emotion.                                                                   

⏺ You're right. "Engineer" and "PM" are job titles that mean completely
  different things at different org sizes. The current model breaks the moment
  you apply it to a solopreneur or a consultant.

  The problem is we're modeling roles when we should be modeling modes.

  ---
  The insight: role is the wrong abstraction

  A solopreneur is an engineer AND a PM AND an admin — simultaneously, sometimes
   in the same hour. Calling them "Root" is meaningless because they're also
  Finch and they're also setting up the whole system.

  A consultant is technically an "engineer" but their relationship with The
  Machine is completely different from a product engineer who built the
  codebase. The consultant needs the Machine to ramp them up on an unfamiliar
  org's conventions. The product engineer needs the Machine to execute faster on
   something they already know deeply.

  A junior engineer and a staff engineer are both "engineers" but:
  - Junior: trust gates are learning moments. They want to understand why, not
  just approve.
  - Staff: mostly reviews and unblocks. Uses the Machine for leverage, not
  execution.

  A PM at a 10-person startup is the person deciding what to build, writing the
  code spec, talking to customers, and approving their own PRDs. A PM at
  Microsoft owns one feature across 50 engineers and spends most of their day in
   alignment meetings.

  Same title. Completely different relationship with The Machine.

  ---
  The right abstraction: modes, not roles

  People don't have a fixed relationship with The Machine. They shift between
  modes depending on what they're doing at that moment.

  MODE          WHAT YOU'RE DOING           WHO IS DOING IT
  ──────────    ──────────────────────      ────────────────────────────────
  BUILDER       Creating, executing,        Engineer, designer, data scientist,
                writing, building           consultant, solopreneur-as-maker

  DECIDER       Reviewing, approving,       PM, manager, senior engineer,
                directing, signing off      legal, solopreneur-as-product-lead

  COORDINATOR   Aligning, tracking,         PM, EM, chief of staff,
                unblocking, visibility      solopreneur-as-CEO

  OPERATOR      Setting up, governing,      Admin, CTO, security engineer,
                maintaining, auditing       solopreneur-as-ops

  The solopreneur shifts between all four modes in the same day.
  A large-org PM lives primarily in Decider + Coordinator.
  A consultant is a Builder in an unfamiliar context.
  A junior engineer is a Builder who is also Learning.

  ---
  How this maps across the spectrums

                  BUILDER     DECIDER     COORDINATOR    OPERATOR
  SOLO            ████████    ████████    ████████       ████████
  (wears all      all day     all day     all day        all day
  hats)

  STARTUP         ████████    ████░░░░    ██░░░░░░       ██░░░░░░
  ENGINEER        heavy       some        light          rare

  STARTUP PM      ████░░░░    ████████    ████████       ░░░░░░░░
                  some        heavy       heavy          none

  CONSULTANT      ████████    ░░░░░░░░    ░░░░░░░░       ░░░░░░░░
                  heavy       (client     (client        (client
                  (on client  approves)   coordinates)   configures)
                  codebase)

  LARGE-ORG       ████████    ████░░░░    ░░░░░░░░       ░░░░░░░░
  ENGINEER        heavy       some        rare           none

  LARGE-ORG PM    ░░░░░░░░    ████████    ████████       ░░░░░░░░
                  none        heavy       very heavy     none

  CTO/ADMIN       ░░░░░░░░    ████░░░░    ████████       ████████
                  none        some        heavy          heavy

  ---
  JTBDs per mode — these hold across org sizes and titles

  BUILDER mode:
  - When I start a task, I want full context assembled so I don't spend 30
  minutes gathering it — whether I've been on this codebase for 3 years or 3
  days.
  - When I'm in flow, I want the Machine to handle coordination so I never break
   focus to update Jira or ping a reviewer.
  - When I'm new to this codebase (consultant, new hire, new team), I want the
  Machine to teach me the conventions as I work — not make me read
  documentation.
  - When the Machine is doing something I disagree with, I want to redirect it
  mid-execution, not start over.
  - When the Machine makes a mistake, I want to correct it in a way it learns
  from.

  DECIDER mode:
  - When I need to approve something, I want enough context to decide
  confidently — not a wall of technical detail I can't parse.
  - When I'm approving something I didn't ask for (background execution), I want
   to understand what triggered it and why the Machine thinks it's right.
  - When the Machine proposes something wrong, I want to reject it with a reason
   that improves future proposals.
  - When I'm not available, I want decisions queued in priority order — not
  dropped and not executed without me.

  COORDINATOR mode:
  - When I need to know where things stand, I want The Machine to brief me
  without me asking anyone — no pinging engineers for status.
  - When a dependency is at risk, I want to know before it becomes a blocker.
  - When I need people to align, I want the Machine to prepare the context so
  the meeting takes 20 minutes not 3 hours.
  - When I'm a solopreneur coordinating between my own past and future
  decisions, I want the trace to be the coordination layer.

  OPERATOR mode:
  - When I introduce The Machine to my team, I want to start conservative —
  Machine suggests, humans always approve — and gradually expand autonomy as
  trust builds.
  - When someone new joins (or a consultant arrives), I want their initial trust
   level to be appropriately cautious and evolve as they build a track record.
  - When the Machine does something unexpected, I want an immediate pause on all
   autonomous action — a kill switch that doesn't lose in-progress state.
  - When something goes wrong, I want a complete, human-readable audit trail
  that a non-engineer can read.
  - When I add a new department (sales joins after EPD), I want complete
  isolation — adding them can't touch what EPD has already set up.

  ---
  The fifth mode: Learner

  This cuts across all the others:

  LEARNER (anyone in week 1 — junior engineer, consultant, new hire, PM new to
  technical work):
  - When a trust gate fires, I want to understand WHY the Machine is pausing —
  not just approve/reject. This is how I learn the org's conventions.
  - When the Machine executes a playbook, I want to read what it did step by
  step — it's teaching me how this team works.
  - When I make a wrong call on a trust gate, I want to understand the
  consequence before it happens.

  Learner mode is temporary — it fades as someone builds context. But it's the
  most important mode for adoption. If a junior engineer or consultant can ramp
  up on a new codebase by watching The Machine work, that's a massive,
  demonstrable value.

  ---
  What this means for the People section in domain-model.md

  Replace the three fixed personas (Engineer, PM, Admin) with:
  1. Four modes with JTBDs that hold across org sizes and titles
  2. A fifth mode (Learner) for onboarding/ramp-up
  3. A mapping showing how people distribute across modes at different org sizes
  4. Examples: how a solopreneur, consultant, junior engineer, staff engineer,
  large-org PM each use The Machine

  The PoI naming (Root/Finch/Admin) stays for trust levels in the system — but
  the product-facing model uses modes, not titles. Root just means "Builder-mode
   trust." Finch means "Decider-mode access." These are access levels, not
  identity.

  Should I rewrite the People section now with this model?

⏺ This is several connected insights. Let me work through all of them together
  because they're actually one unified idea.

  ---
  1-1 vs 1-many — the answer is 1-many, but it's contextual

  One person, many modes. The Machine infers which mode they're in from context
  — opening CLI signals Builder, opening web feed signals Decider, touching
  settings signals Operator. The system doesn't ask "what mode are you in?" — it
   observes.

  But you're pointing at something deeper: at different org sizes the same human
   covers different spans. A solopreneur is 1 person → all modes. A junior
  engineer at Microsoft is 1 person → Builder only. The Machine needs to know
  the expected span of a person's modes, not just their current mode. That's
  exactly what a job description encodes.

  ---
  The missing entity: Job Description

  You're right — it's completely absent from the platform. And it's load-bearing
   for everything you just described.

  A job description in kloudi is not HR paperwork. It's the operating 
  specification for a role — what modes this person uses, which playbooks they
  execute, what work products they produce, what trust level they start with,
  and what outcomes define success.

  JOB DESCRIPTION
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  name:           Senior Engineer
  department:     Engineering
  org_size:       startup → enterprise (scales with org)

  primary modes:  Builder (heavy), Decider (some)
  secondary:      Coordinator (rare), Operator (never)

  playbooks:      engineering/impl, engineering/review-pr,
                  engineering/write-erd, engineering/deploy

  work products:  PRs, migrations, tests, ERDs, ADRs

  integrations:   GitHub (full), Jira (read+comment), Confluence (read)

  trust defaults: Builder → medium (evolves)
                  Decider → can approve own trust gates only

  jtbds:          [the mode-based JTBDs for their role]

  outcome metrics: PRs merged/week, review turnaround time,
                   trust gate accuracy (approved things that worked)

  Now when someone joins an org using kloudi:
  1. Admin assigns them a job description
  2. Machine seeds their playbook access + trust level from the job description
  3. Morning brief is tailored to their role
  4. Trust gates are calibrated to what their role should auto-approve vs prompt

  ---
  The human minion idea — this is a revenue model, and it fits the architecture

  When an org has a gap (no QA engineer, no designer, no copywriter), The
  Machine currently does nothing. But with job descriptions, The Machine knows
  exactly what that role does and can brief any incoming human instantly —
  because the playbook IS the job knowledge.

  So the model becomes:

  PERMANENT OPERATIVES (team members):
    Human + job description + trust arc
    They build trust over time, Machine gives them more autonomy

  TEMPORARY OPERATIVES (sourced by Machine):
    Human minion + job description + fixed engagement
    Machine briefs them via playbooks
    They execute to org's standards
    Machine tracks quality via execution traces
    Org pays per outcome
    kloudi takes platform fee

  The playbook is what makes this work. You can onboard a temporary QA engineer
  to any org instantly because the playbook tells them everything: how this org
  writes tests, what the quality bar is, what tools they use, what the review
  process is. The Machine is the staffing agency AND the manager AND the quality
   checker.

  Revenue model: per outcome. Per PR reviewed. Per design delivered. Per
  analysis completed. Not per seat — per verified output.

  ---
  Job description connects PLG and SLG

  PLG (product-led):
  User signs up → "what's your role?" → Machine loads their job description →
  seeds their playbooks, configures trust, shows them their relevant feed.
  They're productive in minutes because the job description already defines what
   they need.

  Self-service. No sales touch. The job description IS the onboarding flow.

  SLG (sales-led):
  Sales engineer asks the buyer: "what roles will use kloudi?" → maps to job
  descriptions → "here are the 4 job descriptions we'd set up, here's their
  daily experience, here's how you'd measure value for each." The demo is
  personalized to the buyer's org structure before the first meeting.

  Pricing: per active job description executing playbooks per month. Not per
  seat. Aligns with value — you pay for roles that are actively using The
  Machine, not for licenses that sit idle.

  ---
  User research mapping — job descriptions as the frame

  Right now user research is: "Nitish said X in session 3." That's impossible to
   aggregate.

  With job descriptions as the frame:

  INTERVIEW TAG:    Senior PM, Series B startup, 20 engineers
  INSIGHT:          "I have no idea what the Machine did while I was in
  meetings"
  MAPS TO:          Decider mode JTBD → Feed needs better morning summary
                    Job description: Lead PM → feed visibility gap

  INTERVIEW TAG:    Staff Engineer, enterprise, 500 engineers
  INSIGHT:          "I don't write code anymore, I need to review faster"
  MAPS TO:          Decider mode JTBD → trust gate flow for code reviews
                    Job description: Staff Engineer → reviewer tools gap

  Over 20 interviews you can see: "every PM at a company of 50-200 people says
  the same thing about feed visibility." That's a job-description-level pattern,
   not an individual insight. It tells you which job description's JTBD is
  broken and what to fix.

  ---
  What needs to be added to the model

  New entity: Job Description
  - The operating spec for a role in kloudi
  - Contains: modes, playbooks, work products, trust defaults, outcome metrics
  - Assigned to humans at org onboarding / user invite time
  - Used by PLG (self-serve onboarding), SLG (sales demo), research (insight
  mapping), and human minions (temporary operative briefing)

  Extended People entity:
  - One person → one primary job description + possible secondary modes
  - Trust arc is tracked against the job description's expected outcomes
  - Human operatives are either permanent (employee) or temporary (sourced)

  The human operative model:
  - Permanent: team member, trust evolves over time
  - Temporary: Machine-sourced, paid per outcome, briefed via job description
  playbooks

  ---
  This is one of the most significant structural additions to the model. Job
  descriptions connect org design to trust, to onboarding, to user research, to
  PLG, to SLG, and to the human minion revenue model. It's the entity that was
  missing that makes all of People make sense.

  Should I update domain-model.md with Job Description as a new entity and
  rewrite the People section using modes + job descriptions instead of fixed
  role titles?

⏺ Two corrections landed. Let me address both.

  ---
  Mode inference from surface is wrong — here's why

  I made a category error. I assigned modes to people and then tried to infer
  from surface. Both are wrong.

  Modes belong to actions, not people.

  Every action in kloudi is a Builder action, a Decider action, a Coordinator
  action, or an Operator action — regardless of who takes it or from where.

  ACTION                              MODE        SURFACE IT CAN HAPPEN FROM
  ──────────────────────────────────  ──────────  ────────────────────────────
  kloudi run engineering/impl         Builder     CLI or web terminal
  Approve a feed card                 Decider     Web or CLI (trust gate)
  View org-wide execution feed        Coordinator Web
  Change trust levels for a teammate  Operator    Web settings

  The job description defines which action types a person is permitted to take —
   nothing to do with surface. A senior engineer with Builder + Decider
  permissions can run SOPs from CLI or approve from web. A PM with Decider +
  Coordinator permissions can approve from CLI if they want to. The surface is
  just rendering preference.

  So the corrected model:

  JOB DESCRIPTION → defines permitted action types (modes)
  ACTION          → determines current mode
  SURFACE         → irrelevant to mode, just how it's rendered

  A PM using the CLI is still in Decider mode when they're approving something.
  An engineer opening the web feed is still in Builder mode when they run a SOP
  from wterm. The surface never determines the mode.

  ---
  Marketplace — yes, and it's bigger than V5

  The marketplace is mentioned in the docs — CEO Review Decision #6, V5, framed
  as "cross-org SOP sharing." But you're right that it should be thought about
  more fundamentally and earlier, because both SOPs and job descriptions are 
  marketplace assets.

  Think about what a new org needs to get started:

  WITHOUT MARKETPLACE:                 WITH MARKETPLACE:
    Write every SOP from scratch         Download "Series A Engineering Bundle"
    Define every job description         Download "Lead PM at B2B SaaS"
    Guess trust defaults                 Trust defaults come with the JD
    Author every playbook                Playbooks curated and verified

    Weeks of setup                       Running in hours

  This is npm for organizational knowledge. Or the App Store for
  how-your-org-works.

  Three tiers of marketplace assets:

  COMMUNITY (free, open)
    Like open source npm packages
    Any org can publish
    Quality signal: execution count + trace scores

  VERIFIED (paid, curated)
    kloudi-reviewed bundles
    "Engineering @ Series A" — pre-packaged, tested across 100 orgs
    Quality signal: kloudi certification + outcome metrics

  PRIVATE (org-internal)
    Company's proprietary playbooks and job descriptions
    Not shared outside the org
    Large enterprises keep their process IP internal

  Job descriptions in the marketplace:

  "Senior Engineer — Series B SaaS startup"
    ├── 847 installs across orgs
    ├── 4.8/5 quality score
    ├── Comes with: 12 playbooks, GitHub+Jira integration defaults
    ├── Trust defaults: calibrated from 847 orgs' actual trust gate patterns
    └── JTBDs: validated by 2,300 user interviews mapped to this job description

  Every time an org uses a marketplace job description and runs the playbooks,
  the execution traces improve the quality signal for that asset. The more it's
  used, the better it gets. That's the network effect moat.

  Why the data model needs this from day 1:

  The marketplace is V4-V5 to build, but SOPs and job descriptions need these
  fields from V1 or you'll have a migration problem later:

  SOP {
    source:        'org-created' | 'marketplace' | 'machine-generated'
    marketplace_id: string?        ← null until marketplace exists
    version:        string          ← "1.0.0" for versioning
    quality_score:  float?          ← computed from execution traces
    execution_count: int            ← how many times this SOP has run
    is_public:      boolean         ← can be published to marketplace
  }

  JobDescription {
    source:        'org-created' | 'marketplace'
    marketplace_id: string?
    version:        string
    quality_score:  float?
    download_count: int
    is_public:      boolean
  }

  The field is null until the marketplace exists. But the shape is there from
  day 1 so V4 is an activation, not a rewrite.

  PLG + SLG through the marketplace lens:

  PLG:  Org signs up → downloads relevant SOP + JD bundles
        → Machine is running with real playbooks in hours
        → Viral: "where did you get that onboarding playbook?" → from kloudi
  marketplace

  SLG:  Sales engineer shows buyer their industry's SOP bundle
        → "here's what 200 companies like yours are running"
        → Buyer sees immediate value before signing
        → Enterprise deal = private marketplace tier + custom playbooks

  ---
  Now updating domain-model.md with: modes as action-types (not person-types),
  job descriptions as a first-class entity, marketplace as the distribution
  model for both SOPs and JDs.

          +ate kill switch that doesn't lose in-progress state.          
      322 +- When I add a new department, I want complete isolation — add
          +ing them cannot touch what EPD already set up.                
      323 +                                                              
      324 +**Learner mode** (anyone new — junior engineer, consultant, ne
          +w hire, week 1):                                              
      325 +- When a trust gate fires, I want to understand WHY the Machin
          +e is pausing — this is how I learn the org's conventions.     
      326 +- When the Machine executes a playbook, I want to read what it
          + did step by step — it's teaching me how this team works.     
      327 +- When I make a wrong call on a trust gate, I want to understa
          +nd the consequence before it happens.                         
      328 +                                                              
      329 +Learner mode is temporary. It fades as someone builds context 
          +and their trust arc evolves.                                  
      330 +                                                              
      331 +### How it evolves (L1 → L4)                                  
      332 +- L1: Job descriptions manually created, trust manually config
          +ured                                                          
      333 +- L2: Machine suggests JD based on what it observes the person
          + doing                                                        
      334 +- L3: Trust evolves automatically from action history against 
          +JD outcome metrics                                            
      335 +- L4: Machine routes decisions to the right person based on ex
          +pertise, availability, and track record                       
      336 +

⏺ Three things landed:

  Modes corrected. They belong to actions, not people, not surfaces. A PM on CLI
   approving a trust gate is in Decider mode. An engineer on web running a SOP
  is in Builder mode. The surface is irrelevant — it's what you're doing that
  determines the mode. Job descriptions define which action types a person is
  permitted to take.

  Job Description added as a first-class entity. It's the operating spec for a
  role — modes permitted, playbooks, work products, trust defaults, outcome
  metrics. It has marketplace fields from day 1 (null until V4) so there's no
  migration needed when marketplace ships. It also enables the human minion
  model: Machine sources a temporary operative, briefs them via the JD, they're
  immediately productive.

  Marketplace added as entity #10. SOPs and Job Descriptions are both
  marketplace assets. The network effect: every execution accumulates a quality
  signal, so by the time V4 ships the data already exists to rank assets by
  real-world performance. PLG uses it for self-serve onboarding. SLG uses it as
  the demo ("200 companies like yours run these exact playbooks"). The
  execution_count and quality_score fields exist on every SOP from V1 — they
  just don't display anywhere until V4.

⏺ Both points are right. Let me think through the first one carefully because
  it's structurally significant.

  ---
  JDs ARE SOPs — the unification

  A job description has two parts:

  SOFT SKILLS                          TECHNICAL SKILLS
  ────────────────────────────         ────────────────────────────────
  Communication style                  Playbooks they execute
  Decision-making values               Tools they have access to
  Escalation behavior                  Trust defaults per action type
  Collaboration patterns               Work products they produce
  What they prioritize under pressure  Outcome metrics

  In an AI-native world:
  - Soft skills → agent persona — the tone, values, and reasoning style the
  Machine adopts when acting in this role
  - Technical skills → agent capabilities — which playbooks it runs, which tools
   it uses, what trust level it operates at

  So a Role SOP is just a SOP with a different document structure. The Machine
  reads it like any other SOP. The graph derived from it has two node types:

  Document: senior-engineer.md
  Graph (.senior-engineer.graph.json):
    persona_nodes:     communication style, values, escalation → LLM system
  prompt
    capability_nodes:  playbooks, tools, trust defaults → agent configuration

  When The Machine acts as "PM for this sprint" — it reads the PM Role SOP,
  loads the soft skills as its persona, loads the technical skills as its
  playbook access. The Machine IS the PM. When a human fills the role, the same
  document sets their expectations and the Machine calibrates to their behavior.

  This means the three SOP subtypes are:

  PROCESS SOPs    (playbooks/)   — how we do things
    engineering/impl.md
    product/draft-prd.md

  ARTIFACT SOPs   (projects/)    — what we produce
    beacon/prd/v2.md
    beacon/erd/schema.md

  ROLE SOPs       (roles/)       — who does what, how they work, agent persona
    engineering/senior-engineer.md
    product/lead-pm.md
    design/freelance-designer.md   ← for temporary operatives too

  Everything is a SOP. The filesystem convention, the graph derivation, the
  marketplace distribution — all the same machinery.

  ---
  Map existing before marketplace — this principle must be explicit

  You're right that this needs to be clearer. The current onboarding brief has
  the scan-before-propose sequence, but the gap analysis step that connects to
  marketplace isn't explicit enough.

  The correct flow:

  WRONG (what we must not do):
    Org signs up → "here's the Series A bundle, install it"

  RIGHT:
    1. Connect tools
    2. Scan and extract → map existing playbooks + existing roles
    3. GAP ANALYSIS:
         "You have 8 engineering playbooks. Industry average: 14.
          You have roles for 3 people. 2 roles have no SOP yet.
          Missing: engineering/postmortem, product/sprint-planning,
                   role/design/senior-designer"
    4. THEN recommend marketplace assets to fill gaps only
    5. User reviews each recommendation, accepts or rejects
    6. Installs only what fills a real gap

  The gap analysis is what makes marketplace recommendations signal not noise.
  Without it, you're just dumping a bundle and hoping it fits.

  ---
  Updating both the SOP entity and Marketplace entity in domain-model.md:

⏺ Three things now locked:

  JDs are SOPs. Not a separate entity — a third SOP subtype called Role SOPs
  living in roles/. Same filesystem convention, same graph derivation, same
  marketplace distribution. The only difference is what the graph nodes mean:
  soft skills nodes configure the agent persona (LLM system prompt), technical
  skill nodes configure capabilities (playbooks, tools, trust). One Role SOP
  configures both a human operative and an AI agent acting in that role.

  Everything is a SOP. Process SOPs (how), Artifact SOPs (what), Role SOPs
  (who). All the same machinery. All marketplace-distributable. All
  graph-derivable. All quality-scored from execution traces.

  Gap analysis before marketplace. This principle is now explicit in the
  Marketplace entity — scan and map what exists first, identify gaps, then
  recommend only what fills real gaps. The PLG and SLG flows both start with gap
   analysis, not bundle installation.

⏺ Two things in parallel. Starting with V0 since that's actionable now.

  ---
  V0 — what to review

  V0 is what's in the repo today plus 4 bug fixes plus the AI-native engine.
  Three areas to review:

  1. The existing CLI — already working, review for bugs
  - apps/cli/commands/run.ts — WebSocket streaming, trust gates, chalk output.
  Read it and tell me if the UX feels right.
  - apps/cli/commands/init.ts — has 3 known bugs (no Membership,
  --accept-data-loss, seed literal). Review the init flow end to end.
  - data/seed/sops/ — the 5 seed SOPs. Are they actually authored well enough to
   prove anything, or are they placeholder JSON?

  2. The 4 bugs to fix before V0 is usable
  All in TODOS.md P0/P1. Each is a few lines of code. The most important:
  - kloudi init creates org but no membership → user can't auth
  - --accept-data-loss in init → dangerous, remove before running on any real DB

  3. The AI-native engine — the main V0 build
  packages/core/execution/execution-engine.ts is the file to replace. The design
   is in docs/current/agent-loop-design.md. This is the biggest piece of V0.
  Everything else in V0 already exists.

  V0 is done when: kloudi run engineering/impl takes a real Jira ticket and
  creates a real PR, with trust gates working, on your actual codebase.

  ---
  Signup + wow experience

  The wow moment has one definition: The Machine does real work before you've 
  configured anything complicated.

  Not a demo. Not synthetic data. Your actual ticket. Your actual repo. Real PR.

  The time to wow target: under 10 minutes from landing on the signup page.

  ---
  The game structure

  Four levels. Each has a clear goal, immediate feedback, and a small win before
   the big one.

  LEVEL 0  "Meet The Machine"         90 seconds
  LEVEL 1  "Give me eyes"             3 minutes
  LEVEL 2  "Watch me work"            5 minutes  ← THE WOW
  LEVEL 3  "I'll keep watching"       passive
  LEVEL 4  "Bring your team"          2 minutes  ← multiplayer unlocked

  ---
  Level 0 — Meet The Machine (90 seconds)

  No forms. No feature lists. No pricing page. Just a blank screen and The
  Machine speaking first.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                             │
  │   I'm The Machine.                                          │
  │                                                             │
  │   I learn how your org works — then I do the work.         │
  │   Not a chatbot. Not a script runner.                       │
  │   An intelligence that joins your team.                     │
  │                                                             │
  │   Let's start. What do you call your org?                   │
  │                                                             │
  │   > _                                                       │
  │                                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  One field. Org name. Org created. Move to Level 1.

  No email required yet — email capture happens at Level 2 right before the wow
  (right before the PR is created, so there's stakes).

  ---
  Level 1 — Give me eyes (3 minutes)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Level 1 of 4 — Give me eyes                               │
  │                                                             │
  │  I need to see where your team's work lives.               │
  │  Connect one tool to start.                                 │
  │                                                             │
  │  [ Connect GitHub ]    [ Connect Jira ]                    │
  │                                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  User connects GitHub via OAuth. Machine immediately scans.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✓ Level 1 complete                                         │
  │                                                             │
  │  I can see:                                                 │
  │  · 4 repos                                                  │
  │  · 12 open PRs                                              │
  │  · 3 tickets assigned to you                                │
  │                                                             │
  │  ACME-456: "Add SQL safety pre-check" — assigned to you    │
  │  Last touched 2 days ago. No PR yet.                        │
  │                                                             │
  │  Want me to work on this one?  [ Yes ]  [ Pick another ]   │
  │                                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ---
  What makes it feel like a game

  1. Numbered levels with progress indicator — you always know where you are
  2. Machine speaks first at every step — it's active, not waiting
  3. Level complete moment — checkmark, summary, what was accomplished
  4. "Mission complete" screen — the PR is the achievement. Time shown. Real
  link.
  5. Skip options — Level 4 has a skip. Nothing is forced.
  6. Transparency before action — the plan is shown before execution, making the
   user feel in control
  7. First value before account creation — org name only to start. Email
  captured at the moment of maximum motivation (right before the PR)

  ---
  The role of Role SOPs in onboarding

  Level 0.5 (optional, shows after org name):

  "What's your role in your org?"
  [ Engineer ]  [ PM / Manager ]  [ Founder ]  [ Other ]

  One click. Machine loads the right Role SOP. The playbooks, trust defaults,
  and morning brief are immediately calibrated to that role. A PM sees a
  different Level 1 than an engineer — PM sees open PRs needing review, engineer
   sees open tickets.

  This is V1 enhancement — V0 can skip it and default to engineer.

❯ read through the code path and come up with a possible v0. you can say that

⏺ Reading everything now — execution engine, routes, CLI, schema, seed data, web

⏺ Read everything. Here's the honest assessment.

  ---
  What's actually there

  Infrastructure — solid, keep as-is:

  The WebSocket server is well built. Trust gate flow works end-to-end: CLI
  subscribes to execution → engine calls requestTrustGateApproval → broadcasts
  user.ask to all subscribers → CLI prompts user → sends user.ask.response →
  WebSocket resolves the promise → execution resumes. This is correct and
  complete.

  Auth routes are solid. Register creates user + org + membership in one
  transaction. Login looks up membership correctly. The auth P1 bug is only in
  apps/cli/commands/init.ts, not in the web auth flow — so web registration
  works fine today.

  SopService, SopRepository, Prisma schema — all clean. The CRUD layer is done
  and correct.

  The execution engine — works but wrong architecture:

  The engine dispatches on node.type:
  - LLMExecutor: takes prompt_template, calls aiClient.generateText(). LLM is
  called inside an executor. Not AI-native.
  - ToolCallExecutor: looks up tool by name in a registry, calls
  registry.execute(name, params). Static dispatch.
  - InterpolativeExecutor: generates text, picks from a list of options. Not
  AI-native.
  - SubEntityExecutor: calls another SOP recursively. Works correctly.

  The ContextManager is actually decent — budget management, LRU eviction, token
   counting. It assembles context items into a string for LLM calls. Salvageable
   for the AI-native engine with minimal changes.

  The 3 seed SOPs — old schema, one blocking issue:

  Only 3 SOPs exist: engineering-impl, engineering-ship, shared-standup. Not 5.

  engineering-impl.json is well authored — a real 9-node workflow: fetch story →
   read spec → choose approach → create branch → generate plan → implement →
  test → assess → PR. The logic is right. The interpolative branching is
  thoughtful.

  One blocking issue: node implement_code calls tool code_editor with action
  apply_plan. That tool does not exist in the codebase. Engineering-impl will
  fail at that step every time.

  The projection system — good infrastructure, underused:

  skill-to-graph.ts converts SKILL.md markdown → graph JSON. sop-to-skill.ts
  converts graph → SKILL.md. This is the Projection Engine from the design. It
  works and is tested. Useful — but the old node types it produces
  (llm_generate, tool_call, interpolative) will need to change when the
  AI-native schema lands.

  The import system — old decisions:

  skill-to-graph.ts classifies sections based on bash code blocks and decision
  points. Works for the gstack skill format. This is how the 44 Flywl skills
  would be imported manually — but this is superseded by Onboarding Mode
  eventually.

  ---
  The 4 confirmed bugs

  1. P0: Credentials plain JSON — packages/core/prisma/schema.prisma line 114:
  credentials Json @default("{}"). Comment says // plain JSON for now. Not
  encrypted.
  2. P1: CLI ls/trace no auth — apps/cli/commands/ls.ts creates KloudiClient
  with no auth token. Same in trace. Will 401.
  3. P1: kloudi init no Membership — apps/cli/commands/init.ts creates
  organization but no membership. The web auth route DOES create membership
  correctly, so this is init-CLI-specific.
  4. P1: --accept-data-loss — apps/cli/commands/init.ts line with prisma db push
   --accept-data-loss.

  ---
  The real V0 question

  The infrastructure is more solid than I expected. The engine works — it's just
   the wrong architecture. The question is whether V0 needs the AI-native engine
   or can ship with the existing one.

  The blocking issue for V0 is not the engine architecture. It's the missing 
  code_editor tool.

  Right now engineering-impl will fail at node 6 (implement_code) because
  code_editor doesn't exist. Before any V0 demo works, that node needs to be
  replaced with real tools — Write, Edit, Bash from the builtin tool registry.

  Two paths:

  PATH A — V0 with existing engine (2-3 weeks):
    Fix 4 bugs
    Replace code_editor node with real Write/Edit/Bash tools
    Connect real GitHub MCP (create PR, read repo files)
    Connect real Jira MCP (read ticket, update status)
    Author 2 missing SOPs (only 3 exist, need 5)
    Ship: kloudi run engineering-impl → real PR from real ticket
    EXISTING deterministic engine does the work
    Then: AI-native rewrite before V1

  PATH B — V0 with AI-native engine (6-8 weeks):
    Fix 4 bugs
    Rewrite execution engine from agent-loop-design.md
    Rewrite SOP node schema (context_sources, description)
    Rewrite all seed SOPs in new schema
    Connect real MCP tools
    Ship

  Recommendation: Path A. The UX that makes V0 valuable — CLI streaming, trust
  gates, web feed — is entirely independent of whether the engine is
  deterministic or AI-native. A user watching kloudi run engineering-impl create
   a real PR won't know or care which engine ran it.

  Ship V0 with the existing engine. Prove "ticket in → PR out" works. Then do
  the AI-native rewrite as V0.5 before V1. The rewrite is invisible to users and
   the infrastructure doesn't change.

  ---
  V0 task list — in order

  1. Fix --accept-data-loss in kloudi init (30 min)
  2. Add Membership creation to kloudi init (30 min)
  3. Add auth token to CLI ls + trace (1 hour)
  4. Replace code_editor node in engineering-impl.json with
     Write/Edit/Bash tool calls (2 hours)
  5. Wire real GitHub MCP: read_file, create_pr, list_prs (1 day)
  6. Wire real Jira MCP: get_issue, update_issue (half day)
  7. Author engineering-review-pr.json seed SOP (2 hours)
  8. Author shared-standup.json verify it's usable (30 min)
  9. Test kloudi run engineering-impl end-to-end on a real ticket
  10. Credential encryption (2 hours — do before any real creds stored)

  Parallel track: AI-native engine rewrite. Doesn't block V0. Starts once V0 is
  running.

⏺ Path B. Build the AI-native engine.

  Here's why Path A collapses under scrutiny: the code_editor tool replacement
  is not a simple swap. You'd need to build a tool that reads an implementation
  plan (text) and writes actual files — which is essentially half the AI-native
  engine. Then you'd do the AI-native rewrite anyway. Then you'd rewrite all the
   SOPs a second time to the new schema. Path A is actually more total work,
  plus it leaves a transitional codebase with two competing architectures that
  confuses anyone who picks it up.

  The AI-native engine is the product premise. V0 without it isn't kloudi.

  ---
  Three decisions blocking the build start

  1. MCP tool strategy — decide before touching the engine

  The AI-native engine calls tools via the MCP Tool Registry. For V0 you need
  GitHub and Jira. Two options:

  OPTION A: Use official MCP servers
    @modelcontextprotocol/server-github  (exists, MIT)
    @modelcontextprotocol/server-jira    (exists or similar)
    Wire them via stdio MCP in the Tool Registry
    Pro: don't build tool implementations
    Con: dependency on external servers, less control

  OPTION B: Build thin MCP tools directly
    packages/tools/github.ts → thin wrapper over GitHub REST API
    packages/tools/jira.ts   → thin wrapper over Jira REST API
    Wire as builtin tools in the Tool Registry
    Pro: full control, no external dependency
    Con: 1-2 days of work per integration

  This decision gates everything. The engine needs to know what tools exist
  before you can author or test a single SOP.

  2. SOP authoring format for V0 — markdown or JSON

  The design says SOPs live as markdown files. But for V0 (single engineer, no
  filesystem sync, no web editor), storing them as JSON in Postgres is simpler.

  OPTION A: Markdown → derive graph (the design vision)
    author engineering-impl.md
    Machine derives .engineering-impl.graph.json
    Store graph in Postgres
    Pro: aligned with long-term design
    Con: requires a graph derivation step before V0 can run

  OPTION B: JSON directly in Postgres (V0 shortcut)
    author engineering-impl.json with new node schema
    seed directly into Postgres
    No markdown, no filesystem yet
    Pro: ship V0 in days not weeks
    Con: throws away seed data when markdown format lands

  For V0 — Option B. The markdown derivation is V1 (when the Editor app ships
  and generates .graph.json). V0 seeds JSON directly. Accept the throwaway.

  3. Team size right now

  The V0 task list is scoped for 1 focused engineer. If it's more than that,
  tasks can parallelize:

  SOLO:
    Sequential — 3-4 weeks
    Build engine, then wire tools, then author SOPs, then fix bugs

  2 ENGINEERS:
    Engineer 1: AI-native engine + bug fixes (2 weeks)
    Engineer 2: GitHub MCP + Jira MCP + seed SOPs (2 weeks)
    Parallel — ships in 2 weeks

  ---
  V0 build order once decisions are made

  WEEK 1:
    Delete dead code (executors, decisions, patterns)
    Fix 4 bugs (--accept-data-loss, membership, auth headers, credentials)
    Write new SopNode schema in shared/types.ts
    Build AI-native inner loop (execution-engine.ts replacement)
      — just the inner loop, no outer loop, no Observer
    Wire Vercel AI SDK generateText as the LLM call layer

  WEEK 2:
    Wire GitHub MCP (create_pr, read_file, list_prs)
    Wire Jira MCP (get_issue, update_issue)
    Wire builtin tools (read_file, write_file, bash)
    Author engineering-impl.json in new node schema

  WEEK 3:
    Author engineering-review-pr.json
    Author shared-standup.json (verify)
    Test kloudi run engineering-impl end-to-end on a real ticket
    Trust gates verified in CLI
    Web feed shows execution card

  DONE: kloudi run engineering-impl → real PR

⏺ Build clean. 12/12 tasks successful. Committed as 373da1b.

  Here's what just happened in one pass — 3,472 lines deleted, 211 lines added:

  Deleted:
  - 4 executor files (LLMExecutor, ToolCallExecutor, InterpolativeExecutor,
  SubEntityExecutor)
  - execution-engine.ts — the old deterministic engine
  - decisions/ — dead weight, gone
  - patterns/ — dead weight, gone
  - 2 tests for the deleted engine

  Cleaned:
  - Decision + Pattern models dropped from schema
  - Sop schema: removed parentEntityId hierarchy (old design), added marketplace
   fields (source, version, qualityScore, isPublic, marketplaceId) and
  triggeredBy on Execution
  - packages/core/index.ts — updated barrel, no more decisions/patterns imports
  - packages/core/execution/index.ts — no more executor exports
  - apps/api/routes/executions.routes.ts — placeholder with TODO, routes for
  list/get/cancel/activity intact

  Rewritten:
  - All 3 seed SOPs now use the new node schema (description, context_sources,
  available_tools, trust_required). No more code_editor. No more type: 
  llm_generate. LLM receives the description and acts.

  The codebase now has one architecture. The AI-native engine has a clear
  insertion point: packages/core/execution/ with the slot in
  executions.routes.ts ready.
